{\mathcal{B}}
in Section
7
but let us keep notation different since the latter was acting on functions of
(v,v_{*})
and the current one on functions of just one variable. The operator
L_{\beta}
acts on all
L^{p}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})
, in particular on
L^{2}
. Thanks to the exchange
k\cdot\sigma
,

\displaystyle\langle L_{\beta}f,g\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}

\displaystyle=\iint[f(\sigma)-f(k)]\,g(k)\,\beta(k\cdot\sigma)\,d\sigma\,dk

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\iint[f(\sigma)-f(k)]\,[g(\sigma)-g(k)]\,\beta(k\cdot\sigma)\,d\sigma\,dk.

So
L_{\beta}
is self-adjoint and nonpositive,

(11.3)

-\langle L_{\beta}f,f\rangle_{L^{2}}=\frac{1}{2}\iint_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}[f(\sigma)-f(k)]^{2}\,\beta(k\cdot\sigma)\,dk\,d\sigma.

Further, along
e^{tL_{\beta}}
all convex integrals decay: if
C
is any differentiable convex function, bounded below by a linear function, then
{\mathcal{C}}(f)=\int C(f)\,d\sigma
satisfies

\displaystyle{\mathcal{C}}(f)\cdot L_{\beta}f

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}C^{\prime}(f(k))[f(\sigma)-f(k)]\,\beta(k\cdot\sigma)\,dk\,d\sigma

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\iint_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\Bigl(C^{\prime}(f(\sigma))-C^{\prime}(f(k))\Bigr)\bigl(f(\sigma)-f(k)\bigr)\leq 0.

Of course the most famous dissipation on the sphere is the heat equation, whose generator, the Laplace–Beltrami operator
\Delta
, may be introduced in several ways. For instance,
\Delta f
at
x
is the usual Laplacian of
f(x/|x|)
, evaluated at
x
. Or
\Delta=\sum_{ij}(x_{i}\partial_{j}-x_{j}\partial_{i})^{2}/2
(an average of “pure” second derivatives along rotations in the planes
(i,j)
, thus yielding
\Delta
in any dimension from
\Delta
in dimension 1). In any case, the explicit expression in coordinates is

\displaystyle\Delta f

\displaystyle=\sum_{ij}(\delta_{ij}-x_{i}x_{j})\frac{\partial{}^{2}f}{\partial x_{i}\,\partial x_{j}}-(d-1)\,x\cdot\nabla f

\displaystyle=\mathop{{\rm tr}\,}(\Pi_{x^{\bot}}\nabla^{2}f)-(d-1)\,x\cdot\nabla f,

where
\nabla^{2}
and
\nabla
are usual differential operators in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and
f
is extended outside
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
in any way. Then
\Delta
is also self-adjoint and

-\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}(\Delta f)g\,d\sigma=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\nabla f\cdot\nabla g\,d\sigma.

In the sequel,
\nabla
and
\nabla^{2}
will be the intrinsic gradient and Hessian on the sphere: when
f
is 0-homogeneous (
f=f(x/|x|)
) they coincide with the usual expressions in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
; when defined in an intrinsic way,
\nabla f(\sigma)
is always orthogonal to
\sigma
and
\nabla^{2}f
is a symmetric operator on
\sigma^{\bot}
.

The usual
\Gamma
procedure yields

Proposition 11.1
.

If
\Delta
is the Laplace operator on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
then (
8.1
) and (
8.2
) reduce to the following expressions, for any
f:\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\to\mathbb{R}
:

\Gamma(f)=\Gamma(f,f)=|\nabla f|^{2},

which will be denoted also
\Gamma_{1}(f)
or
\Gamma_{1}(f,f)
; and

\Gamma_{2}(f)=\Gamma_{2}(f,f)=\Delta\frac{|\nabla f|^{2}}{2}-\nabla f\cdot\nabla\Delta f=\|\nabla^{2}f\|_{\rm HS}^{2}+(d-2)|\nabla f|^{2}

(a particular case of Bochner’s formula).

Note carefully, the expressions above only apply to a function defined on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
; if
f
is defined on, say,
\mathbb{R}^{d}
then for instance
\nabla f(\sigma)
should be replaced by
\Pi_{\sigma^{\bot}}\nabla f(\sigma)
, the orthogonal component of the gradient.

It turns out that
\Delta
is part of the Boltzmann family on the sphere; not surprisingly, its limit in the asymptotics of grazing collisions. Let us convene that a sequence
(\beta_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
concentrates on grazing collisions if
\mu_{n}=\int(1-k\cdot\sigma)\beta_{n}(k\cdot\sigma)\,d\sigma
has a finite limit
\mu>0
, while for any
\theta_{0}>0
,
\int 1_{k\cdot\sigma\leq\cos\theta_{0}}\beta_{n}(k\cdot\sigma)\,d\sigma\longrightarrow 0
. (Here
k
is any fixed unit vector.) Then

Proposition 11.2
.

(i) For any two kernels
\beta_{0}
and
\beta_{1}
,
[L_{\beta_{0}},L_{\beta_{1}}]=0
;

(ii) In the AGC, if
\int(1-k\cdot\sigma)\beta(k\cdot\sigma)\longrightarrow\mu>0
, then

L_{\beta}\longrightarrow\left(\frac{\mu}{d-1}\right)\Delta;

(iii) For any kernel
\beta
,
[L_{\beta},\Delta]=0
.

Proof of Proposition
11.2
.

We start with (ii), which follows the same pattern as the AGC for Boltzmann, using
k\cdot\sigma=\cos\theta
, and the identities (
5.12
) and (
5.13
). As
\theta\simeq 0
,

f(\sigma)-f(k)\simeq(\sigma-k)\cdot\nabla f(k)+\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{2}f(k)\cdot(\sigma-k,\sigma-k)\\
=\left(-\frac{\theta^{2}}{2}k+\theta\phi\right)\cdot\nabla f(k)+\frac{1}{2}\bigl\langle\nabla^{2}f(k)\cdot\phi,\phi\bigr\rangle\theta^{2}+o(\theta^{2}).

So from Lemma
5.4
,

\displaystyle\int[f(\sigma)-f(k)]\,\beta(k\cdot\sigma)\,d\sigma

\displaystyle=-k\cdot\nabla f(k)\int\frac{\theta^{2}}{2}\,\beta(k\cdot\sigma)\,d\sigma+\left(\int\phi\,\beta(k\cdot\sigma)\,d\sigma\right)\cdot\nabla f(k)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\int\langle\nabla^{2}f(k)\cdot\phi,\phi\rangle\,\frac{\theta^{2}}{2}\,\beta(k\cdot\sigma)\,d\sigma+o(1)

\displaystyle=-k\cdot\nabla f(k)\,\mu+0+\frac{1}{d-1}\mathop{{\rm tr}\,}\bigl(\nabla^{2}f(k)\,\Pi_{k^{\bot}}\bigr)\,\mu+o(1).

Of course (iii) will result from (i) and (ii). So it only remains to check (i). Let us assume that
\int\beta_{0}
,
\int\beta_{1}
are finite; the general case will follow by approximation. Then, for any
\ell\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
,

L_{\beta_{1}}L_{\beta_{0}}f(\ell)=\int\Bigl[(L_{\beta_{0}}f)(k)-(L_{\beta_{0}}f)(\ell)\Bigr]\,\beta_{1}(\ell\cdot k)\,dk\\
=\iint f(\sigma)\,\beta_{0}(k\cdot\sigma)\,\beta_{1}(\ell\cdot k)\,dk\,d\sigma-\iint f(k)\,\beta_{0}(k\cdot\sigma)\,\beta_{1}(\ell\cdot k)\,dk\,d\sigma\\
-\iint f(\sigma)\,\beta_{0}(\ell\cdot\sigma)\,\beta_{1}(\ell\cdot k)\,dk\,d\sigma+\iint f(\ell)\,\beta_{0}(\ell\cdot\sigma)\,\beta_{1}(\ell\cdot k)\,dk\,d\sigma.

Let us try to exchange
\beta_{0}
and
\beta_{1}
in the latter expression made up of four integrals.

The last of the four integrals is the easiest: exchanging
k
and
\sigma
,

\displaystyle f(\ell)\iint\beta_{0}(\ell\cdot\sigma)\,\beta_{1}(\ell\cdot k)\,dk\,d\sigma

\displaystyle=f(\ell)\iint\beta_{0}(\ell\cdot k)\,\beta_{1}(\ell\cdot\sigma)\,dk\,d\sigma

\displaystyle=f(\ell)\iint\beta_{1}(\ell\cdot\sigma)\,\beta_{0}(\ell\cdot k)\,dk\,d\sigma.

Now turn to the first of the four integrals. Here is the trick (which I learnt from Sacha Bobylev) to use in such a situation: For any fixed
\sigma
we may find a change of variables
k\to\mathaccent 869{k}
by an isometry of
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
which exchanges
\sigma
and
\ell
. Then

\displaystyle\int\beta_{0}(k\cdot\sigma)\,\beta_{1}(\ell\cdot k)\,dk

\displaystyle=\int\beta_{0}(\mathaccent 869{k}\cdot\sigma)\,\beta_{1}(\ell\cdot\mathaccent 869{k})\,dk

\displaystyle=\int\beta_{0}(k\cdot\ell)\,\beta_{1}(\sigma\cdot k)\,dk,

whence

\iint f(\sigma)\,\beta_{0}(k\cdot\sigma)\,\beta_{1}(\ell\cdot k)\,dk\,d\sigma=\iint f(\sigma)\,\beta_{1}(k\cdot\sigma)\,\beta_{0}(\ell\cdot k).

Finallly the same trick will apply to
the sum
of the two central integrals:

\displaystyle\iint f(k)\,\beta_{0}(k\cdot\sigma)\,\beta_{1}(\ell\cdot k)\,dk\,d\sigma

\displaystyle+\iint f(\sigma)\,\beta_{0}(\ell\cdot\sigma)\,\beta_{1}(\ell\cdot k)\,dk\,d\sigma

\displaystyle=\iint f(k)\,\beta_{0}(\ell\cdot\sigma)\,\beta_{1}(\ell\cdot k)\,dk\,d\sigma

\displaystyle+\iint f(\sigma)\,\beta_{0}(\ell\cdot\sigma)\,\beta_{1}(\sigma\cdot k)\,dk\,d\sigma

(change of variable:
\sigma
, exchange:
k
&
\ell
)

(c.o.v.
k
, exch.
\ell
&
\sigma
)

\displaystyle=\iint f(\sigma)\,\beta_{0}(\ell\cdot k)\,\beta_{1}(\ell\cdot\sigma)\,dk\,d\sigma

\displaystyle+\iint f(k)\,\beta_{0}(\ell\cdot k)\,\beta_{1}(\sigma\cdot k)\,dk\,d\sigma.

(exchanging
k
and
\sigma
)

(idem)

∎

Now let us go on with the
\Gamma
formalism for
L_{\beta}
and
\Delta
:

Definition 11.3

(Gamma commutators involving linear Boltzmann)
.

Given a Boltzmann kernel
\beta
, let

\Gamma_{\beta}(f,f)=\frac{1}{2}\Bigl(L_{\beta}(f^{2})-2f\,L_{\beta}f\Bigr);

\Gamma_{1,\beta}(f,f)=\frac{1}{2}\Bigl[L_{\beta}\,\Gamma_{1}(f,f)-2\Gamma_{1}(f,L_{\beta}f)\Bigr].

It turns out that by commutation of
L_{\beta}
and
\Delta
, it does not matter in which order we let the two operators act: also

\Gamma_{1,\beta}(f,f)=\frac{1}{2}\Bigl[\Delta\,\Gamma_{\beta}(f,f)-2\Gamma_{\beta}(f,\Delta f)\Bigr].

Explicit computations yield

(11.4)

\Gamma_{\beta}(f,f)(k)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}(f(\sigma)-f(k))^{2}\,\beta(k\cdot\sigma)\,d\sigma

(11.5)

\Gamma_{1,\beta}(f,f)(k)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\bigl|\nabla f(\sigma)-\nabla f(k)\bigr|_{k,\sigma}^{2}\,\beta(k\cdot\sigma)\,d\sigma,

where as before
|y-x|_{k,\sigma}^{2}=|x|^{2}+|y|^{2}-2(P_{k\sigma}x)\cdot y
and

P_{k\sigma}
is defined as in Proposition
8.12
. Notice: since
\nabla f(\sigma)\cdot\sigma=0
, here
M_{\sigma k}\nabla f(\sigma)=P_{\sigma k}\nabla f(\sigma)
.

Eventually there is also a nice formula for
\nabla L_{\beta}
:

(11.6)

\nabla L_{\beta}(f)=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\bigl[P_{\sigma k}\nabla f(\sigma)-\nabla f(k)\bigr]\,\beta(k\cdot\sigma)\,d\sigma.

Further, in the AGC, as a consequence of the commutation,

(11.7)

\Gamma_{1,\beta}(f,f)\xrightarrow[AGC]{}\frac{\mu}{d-1}\,\Gamma_{2}(f,f).

And of course, we may also study the evolution of the Fisher information
I
under the Boltzmann flow and define as in (
8.3
)

(11.8)

\Gamma_{I,L_{\beta}}(f)=L_{\beta}\bigl(f|\nabla\log f|^{2}\bigr)-\left[(L_{\beta}f)|\nabla\log f|^{2}+2\nabla f\cdot\nabla\left(\frac{L_{\beta}f}{f}\right)\right],

so that

(11.9)

-I^{\prime}(f)\cdot L_{\beta}f=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\Gamma_{I,L_{\beta}}(f);

and we find, as in Section
8
and with the natural simplifications,

(11.10)

\Gamma_{I,L_{\beta}}(f)\,(k)=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}f(\sigma)\bigl|\nabla\log f(\sigma)-\nabla\log f(k)\bigr|^{2}_{k,\sigma}\,\beta(k\cdot\sigma)\,d\sigma

a limit case being

(11.11)

\Gamma_{I,\Delta}(f)=2f\,\Gamma_{2}(\log f).

A final subject we should explore is, quite naturally in this linear context, the spectrum. This will deserve a section on its own.

Bibliographical Notes

Linear Boltzmann equations are classical in neutronics
[
47
]
; but also, interacting particle systems are naturally described by a linear Boltzmann equation in a phase space for several particles, shaped by the conservation laws. Kac
[
115
]
took the jist of this approach by letting his “particles” be described by a point in a high-dimensional sphere. But in this section the sphere may be low-dimensional, and as we have seen the linear Boltzmann equation on this space can help understanding the nonlinear Boltzmann equation.

I am not aware of any reference where the subject of this section is explicitly treated, but at least part of it must be folklore. I learnt the important trick for the commutation of the linear Boltzmann equations in Bobylev’s survey paper on the Boltzmann equation with Maxwellian kernels
[
23
]
, he used this to prove the commutation between (nonlinear) spatially homogeneous Maxwellian Boltzmann and Fokker–Planck semigroups. This is a particular case of much more general theorems used in representation theory and spectral analysis.

12.
Apparition spectrale

Eigenfunctions of the Laplace–Beltrami operator constitute an orthonormal basis of
L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})
, generalising to higher dimensions the decomposition of
L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
in Fourier series. These functions are the spherical harmonics on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
and they have been studied and classified for more than two centuries, especially on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
. There are several ways to introduce them, one is that spherical harmonics are restrictions to
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
of homogeneous polynomials. By definition, a polynomial
P:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
is
\ell
-homogeneous (
\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
) if
P(\lambda x)=\lambda^{\ell}P(x)
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\lambda>0
. Then the restriction of
P
to
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
satisfies

\Delta P=\lambda\,P\qquad\lambda=\ell(\ell+d-2).

(Think that
\Delta|x|^{\ell}=\ell(\ell+d-2)
, even though that is a mnemo rather than an example, since the restriction of
|x|^{\ell}
is trivial.) So the eigenfunctions of
-\Delta
are
0,d-1,2d,3(d+1)
, etc.

Spherical harmonics have “explicit’ expressions in terms of special functions: as we see in Wikipedia (why not), they can be written
Y_{\ell,m}^{(d)}(\theta,\phi)
, where
\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
m
is a
(d-2)
-index,
m=(m_{1},\ldots,m_{d-2})
,
|m_{1}|\leq m_{2}\leq\ldots\leq m_{d-2}\leq\ell
, and
x
is represented by its
(d-1)
-dimensional spherical coordinates:
\theta\in[0,\pi]
,
\phi\in\mathbb{S}^{d-2}
through
x=(\cos\theta)e+(\sin\theta)\phi
,
e
fixed arbitrarily,
\phi\in S^{d-2}_{e^{\bot}}
, and recursively
\phi=\phi_{d-2}
,

\phi_{j}=(\cos\psi_{j})e_{j}+(\sin\psi_{j})\phi_{j-1}
with
e_{j}
fixed arbitrarily and
\phi_{j-1}\in\mathbb{S}^{j-1}_{e_{j}^{\bot}}
,
0\leq\psi_{j}\leq\pi
, and in the end
\phi
is identified with its coordinates
(\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{d-2})
. Then, in complex notation,

Y_{\ell,m}(\theta,\phi)=\frac{e^{im_{1}\psi_{1}}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\overline{P}_{m_{2}}^{m_{1},(2)}(\psi_{2})\overline{P}_{m_{3}}^{m_{2},(3)}(\psi_{3})\ldots\overline{P}_{\ell}^{m_{d-2},(d-1)}(\theta),

where

P_{L}^{\ell,(j)}(\psi)=\sqrt{\left(\frac{2L+j-1}{2}\right)\frac{(L+\ell+j-2)!}{(L-\ell)!}}\,(\sin^{\frac{2-j}{2}}\psi)\,P_{L+\frac{j-2}{2}}^{-\left(\ell+\frac{j-2}{2}\right)}(\cos\psi),

and
P_{\lambda}^{\mu}
is Legendre’s special function, one of the solutions of the second-order real singular differential linear equation

(1-x^{2})y^{\prime\prime}(x)-2xy^{\prime}(x)+\left(\lambda(\lambda+1)-\frac{\mu^{2}}{1-x^{2}}\right)y=0

(itself a particular instance of the hypergeometric function); then the associated eigenvalue is
\lambda_{\ell}^{(d)}=\ell(\ell+d-2)
. (Phew!)

I will not need these explicit formulae, and for the sequel the spherical harmonics of given degree
\ell
may be ordered in an arbitrary way. But the following facts about spherical harmonics
Y_{\ell,m}^{(d)}
in dimension
d\geq 2
will be useful. (The case
\ell=0
is omitted: obviously, the eigenspace is just made of constant functions. Note, I write
\mathbb{N}=\{1,2,3,\ldots\}
.)

Proposition 12.1

(Spherical harmonics on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
)
.

(i) For each
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, the spherical harmonics
(Y_{\ell,m}^{(d)})_{1\leq m\leq N(d,\ell)}
constitute an orthonormal basis of
H_{\ell}
, the eigenspace of
-\Delta
associated with the eigenvalue
\lambda_{\ell}=\ell(\ell+d-2)
, with multiplicity

N(d,\ell)=\left(\frac{2\ell+d-2}{\ell}\right)\,\small\left(\!\begin{array}[]{c}\ell+d-3\\
\ell-1\end{array}\!\right);

(ii) For any
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
there is a unique harmonic
\ell
-homogeneous polynomial
P_{\ell}=P_{\ell}^{(d)}
(sometimes denoted
{}_{d-1}\!P_{\ell}^{0}
) on
[-1,1]
such that for all
k,\sigma
in
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
,

P_{\ell}(k\cdot\sigma)=\frac{|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|}{N(d,\ell)}\sum_{m=1}^{N(d,\ell)}Y_{\ell,m}(k)Y_{\ell,m}(\sigma).

This is the addition formula, and
P_{\ell}
is the Legendre polynomial of order
\ell
in dimension
d
. Legendre polynomials contain most of the relevant information about spherical harmonics. In particular, the space of spherical harmonics of order
\ell
which admit an axis of symmetry
e\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
is a one-dimensional space generated by
\sigma\longmapsto P_{\ell}(\sigma\cdot e)
. Similarly, there are vectors
(\eta_{m})_{1\leq m\leq N(d,\ell)}
such that for any
Y\in H_{\ell}
there are coefficients
(a_{m})_{1\leq m\leq N(d,\ell)}
such that one may write

Y(\sigma)=\sum_{m=1}^{N(d,\ell)}a_{m}\,P_{\ell}(\sigma\cdot\eta_{m})
.

(iii) Legendre polynomials
P_{\ell}^{(d)}
are even for even
\ell
and odd for odd
\ell
; they satisfy
P_{\ell}(1)=1
and
|P_{\ell}(x)|\leq 1
for all
x\in[-1,1]
. Moreover they satisfy the following four important identities:

\bullet
Rodrigues formula:

P_{\ell}(x)=\frac{(-1)^{\ell}}{2^{\ell}\left(\ell+\frac{d-3}{2}\right)_{\ell}}(1-x^{2})^{-\left(\frac{d-3}{2}\right)}\frac{d}{dx}^{\ell}\!(1-x^{2})^{\ell+\frac{d-3}{2}},

where
a_{\ell}=a(a-1)\ldots(a-\ell+1)
.

\bullet
Hypergeometric differential equation:

(12.1)

(1-x^{2})P^{\prime\prime}_{\ell}(x)-(d-1)xP^{\prime}_{\ell}(x)+\ell(\ell+d-2)\,P_{\ell}(x)=0.

\bullet
Recursion formula:
P_{0}(x)=1
,
P_{1}(x)=x
, and

(\ell+d-2)P_{\ell+1}(x)-(2\ell+d-2)xP_{\ell}(x)+\ell P_{\ell-1}(x)=0.

\bullet
Representation by axisymmetric averaging:

(12.2)

P_{\ell}^{(2)}(x)=\Re(x+i\sqrt{1-x^{2}})^{\ell}

and, for
d\geq 3
,

(12.3)

P_{\ell}(x)=\frac{|\mathbb{S}^{d-3}|}{|\mathbb{S}^{d-2}|}\int_{-1}^{1}\Bigl(x+i\,s\,\sqrt{1-x^{2}}\Bigl)^{\ell}(1-s^{2})^{(d-4)/2}\,ds.

Example 12.2
.

For
d=2
, an element in
\mathbb{S}^{1}
can be identified to an angle
\theta\in\mathbb{R}/(2\pi\mathbb{Z})
; then
\Delta
is the usual operator
d^{2}/d\theta^{2}
, the eigenspace
H_{\ell}
is
2
-dimensional for any
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
and an orthonormal basis is (
\pi^{-1/2}\cos(n\theta)
,
\pi^{-1/2}\sin(n\theta)
); then the addition formula turns into

P_{\ell}(\cos(\theta^{\prime}-\theta))=\cos(\ell\theta)\cos(\ell\theta^{\prime})+\sin(\ell\theta)\sin(\ell\theta^{\prime}),

which by the usual addition formula for trigonometric functions is just
\cos(\ell(\theta^{\prime}-\theta))
; so
P_{\ell}
is just the Tchebyshev polynomial defined by the identity

P_{\ell}(\cos\alpha)=\cos(\ell\alpha).

In complex notation, we immediately see that
P_{\ell}(\cos\alpha)=\Re e^{i\ell\alpha}=\Re(\cos\alpha+\sqrt{1-\cos^{2}\alpha})^{\ell}
, which amounts to (
12.2
).

Remark 12.3
.

Formula (
12.3
) shows that, in some sense, all
P_{\ell}
can be obtained from the 2-dimensional case; indeed, this formula amounts to writing a harmonic polynomial in two variables, say
x_{1},x_{2}
, and symmetrize it around the axis
e_{1}
. Note that the integral in (
12.3
) is real by symmetry
y\to-y
and the operation applied to the polynomial is indeed an averaging, since

\int_{-1}^{1}{\sqrt{1-s^{2}}\,}^{d-4}\,ds=\int_{0}^{\pi}(\sin\theta)^{d-3}\,d\theta=\frac{|\mathbb{S}^{d-2}|}{|\mathbb{S}^{d-3}|}.

Legendre polynomials have since long been used to describe the spectrum of the linearised Boltzmann equation on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. But they will also be useful to describe the spectrum of the linear Boltzmann equation on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
.

Theorem 12.4

(Spectrum of the linear Boltzmann equation)
.

If a collisional kernel
\beta
is given on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, then the linear operator
-L_{\beta}
has eigenvalues
(\lambda_{\ell})_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
, and for each
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,
H_{\ell}
is an invariant subspace of dimension
N(d,\ell)
associated with
\nu_{\ell}
, where

(12.4)

\displaystyle\nu_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\bigl[1-P_{\ell}^{(d)}(k\cdot\sigma)\bigr]\,\beta(k\cdot\sigma)\,d\sigma

\displaystyle=|\mathbb{S}^{d-2}|\int_{0}^{\pi}\bigl[1-P_{\ell}^{(d)}(\cos\theta)\bigr]\,\beta(\cos\theta)\,\sin^{d-2}\theta\,d\theta.

Remark 12.5
.

It may be that some eigenvalues
\nu_{\ell}
coincide, depending on the choice of
\beta
; so the multiplicity may not be exactly
N(d,\ell)
.

Proof of Theorem
12.4
.

Let me skip the technical issue of domain of definition (handled by many authors in the much more tricky case of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
) and jump directly to the calculation of the spectrum. Since
\Delta
and
L_{\beta}
commute, we know that the basis of spherical harmonics
(Y_{\ell,m})
is a basis of diagonalisation for
L_{\beta}
. The question is to find the associated eigenvalues

\nu(Y)=-\langle L_{\beta}Y,Y\rangle=\frac{1}{2}\iint\bigl[Y(\sigma)-Y(k)\bigr]^{2}\,\beta(k\cdot\sigma)\,dk\,d\sigma.

A particular choice for
Y
is
Y(k)=c\,P_{\ell}(k\cdot\eta)
for some
\eta\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, where
c
is a normalisation constant. If
\eta
and
\zeta
are two such unit vectors, again using an isometry of
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
which exchanges
\eta
and
\zeta
, we find

\frac{1}{2}\iint\bigl[P_{\ell}(\sigma\cdot\eta)-Y(k\cdot\eta)\bigr]^{2}\,\beta(k\cdot\sigma)\,dk\,d\sigma=\frac{1}{2}\iint\bigl[P_{\ell}(\sigma\cdot\zeta)-Y(k\cdot\zeta)\bigr]^{2}\,\beta(k\cdot\sigma)\,dk\,d\sigma;

in other words the eigenvalue
\nu
associated with
P_{\ell}(k\cdot\eta)
is independent of the choice of
\eta
. Since all spherical harmonics
Y
are linear combinations of such elementary functions, we deduce that the eigenvalue
\nu(Y)
is independent of
Y
and only depends on
\ell
. It remains to compute this number.

Consider the elementary Boltzmann equation operator
L^{\theta}
where
\beta
is concentrated at a single deviation angle
\theta
:

L^{\theta}f(k)=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}[f(\sigma)-f(k)]\,1_{k\cdot\sigma=\cos\theta}\,d\sigma.

In this way

(12.5)

L_{\beta}=\int_{0}^{\pi}L^{\theta}\,\beta(\cos\theta)\,\sin^{d-2}\theta\,d\theta.

Each
\sigma\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
such that
k\cdot\sigma=\cos\theta
is the endpoint of a unit-speed geodesic
\gamma:[0,\theta]\to\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
such that
\gamma(0)=k
,
\dot{\gamma}(0)=\phi\in\mathbb{S}^{d-2}_{k^{\bot}}
. In other words,
\sigma=\exp_{k}(\theta\phi)
. Thus

L^{\theta}f(k)=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-2}_{k^{\bot}}}\Bigl[f\bigl(\exp_{k}\theta\phi\bigr)-f(k)\Bigr]\,d\phi.

Again
L^{\theta}
commutes with
\Delta
, and the eigenvalues are given by the numbers

\nu_{\ell}(\theta)=-\langle L_{\theta}Y,Y\rangle,

where
Y
is any normalised spherical harmonic in
H_{\ell}
. Then

\displaystyle\nu_{\ell}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}Y(k)\left(\int_{\mathbb{S}_{k^{\bot}}^{d-2}}\bigl[Y(k)-Y(\exp_{k}\theta\phi)\bigr]\,d\phi\right)dk

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}Y(k)^{2}\,|\mathbb{S}^{d-2}_{k^{\bot}}|\,dk-\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-2}_{k^{\bot}}}Y(k)\,Y(\exp_{k}\theta\phi)\,d\phi\,dk

\displaystyle=|\mathbb{S}^{d-2}|-\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-2}_{k^{\bot}}}Y(k)\,Y(\exp_{k}\theta\phi)\,d\phi\,dk,

and as a consequence
\iint Y(k)Y(\exp_{k}\theta\phi)\,d\phi\,dk
does not depend on the choice of
Y
.

By the addition formula of Proposition
12.1
(ii), for all
k
and
\sigma=\exp_{k}\theta\phi
,

P_{\ell}^{(d)}(k\cdot\sigma)=\frac{|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|}{N(d,\ell)}\sum_{m=1}^{N(d,\ell)}Y_{\ell,m}(k)\,Y_{\ell,m}(\sigma),

so upon integration
d\phi\,dk
and substitution
k\cdot\sigma=\cos\theta
,

P^{(d)}_{\ell}(\cos\theta)\,|\mathbb{S}^{d-2}|\,|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|=\frac{|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|}{N(d,\ell)}\sum_{m=1}^{N(d,\ell)}\iint Y_{\ell,m}(k)\,Y_{\ell,m}(\exp_{k}\theta\phi)\,d\phi\,dk.

On the right hand side, all terms in the sum are equal to
|\mathbb{S}^{d-2}|-\nu_{\ell}(\theta)
. So

P^{(d)}_{\ell}(\cos\theta)\,|\mathbb{S}^{d-2}|\,|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|=|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|\,\bigl(|\mathbb{S}^{d-2}|-\nu_{\ell}(\theta)\bigr).

All in all,

(12.6)

\nu_{\ell}(\theta)=|\mathbb{S}^{d-2}|\,\bigl(1-P^{(d)}_{\ell}(\cos\theta)\bigr).

Then the conclusion follows from (
12.5
) and its corollary
\nu_{\ell}(\beta)=\int_{0}^{\pi}\nu_{\ell}(\theta)\,\beta(\cos\theta)\,\sin^{d-2}\theta\,d\theta
.
∎

Bibliographical Notes

About spherical harmonics, among innumerable references (the majority of which insist on dimension
d=3
) I recommend the exceptionally clear lecture notes by Frye and Efthimiou
[
87
]
considering general dimensions.

The application of special functions and symmetries to the computation of the spectrum of the Boltzmann equation with Maxwellian kernels goes back at least to Wang Chang and Uhlenbeck in the sixties
[
190
]
; general background is in the reference books like Cercignani
[
47
]
. More recently, noting that the relevant material is scattered in a number of places, Dolera
[
74
]
provides a self-contained presentation and review. The eigenvalues are also given by integral expressions involving the kernel and Legendre polynomials.

13.
Reduction to the sphere, reformulated

Using the language of Section
11
it is possible to rewrite the criterion from Section
10
in a more synthetic way. For simplicity I will only consider product collision kernels:

B(v-v_{*},\sigma)=\Phi(|v-v_{*}|)\,\beta(\cos\theta)
; it is not difficult to adapt the statements to more general collision kernels. The associated Landau function
\Psi
is proportional to
|v-v_{*}|^{2}\Phi(|v-v_{*}|)
. Theorem
10.2
, in its simplest version, can now be rewritten

Theorem 13.1

(Criterion for decay of the Fisher information)
.

Consider the spatially homogeneous Boltzmann equation with kernel
B(v-v_{*},\sigma)=\Phi(|v-v_{*}|)\,\beta(\cos\theta)
, or the Landau equation with function
\Psi(|v-v_{*}|)
proportional to
|v-v_{*}|^{2}\Phi(|v-v_{*}|)
. Let

\overline{\gamma}=\sup_{r>0}\frac{r\,|\Phi^{\prime}(r)|}{\Phi(r)}=\sup_{r>0}\left|\frac{r\Psi^{\prime}(r)}{\Psi(r)}-2\right|.

Then, the Fisher information
I
is nonincreasing along solutions of equation, as soon as

\overline{\gamma}\leq\sqrt{K_{*}},

where
K_{*}
is the best constant in the following inequality, required for all
F:\mathbb{S}^{d-1}/\{\pm I\}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
,

\bullet
For Boltzmann:

(13.1)

\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\Gamma_{\beta}(\sqrt{F})\leq\frac{1}{K_{*}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}F\,\Gamma_{1,\beta}(\log F),

where the expressions of
\Gamma_{\beta}
and
\Gamma_{1,\beta}
are in Definition
11.3
;

\bullet
For Landau:

(13.2)

\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\Gamma_{1}(\sqrt{F})\leq\frac{1}{K_{*}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}F\,\Gamma_{2}(\log F),

where the expressions of
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
are in Proposition
11.1
.

The constant
K_{*}
a priori depends on whether we are considering Boltzmann or Landau, and a priori depends on
\beta
. Here
I
is the identity on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
. Note that in the definition of
F
, taking quotient by
\{\pm I\}
is the same as requiring
F
to be even. In differential geometry,
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}/\{\pm I\}
is called the projective space in dimension
d
(that is, the set of lines in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
) and denoted
\mathbb{R}\!\mathbb{P}^{d-1}
.

Obviously these criteria can be refined as in Section
10
, considering non-factorised kernels
B
, but also replacing the square roots by the more precise nonlinearity there. The advantage of the formulation above is that it is compact and synthetic. Clearly (
13.2
) is the AGC of (
13.1
). The remarkable achievement of Guillen–Silvestre is the explicit reduction of the decay of
I
to an optimal functional inequality of logarithmic Sobolev type. Before going more deeply in this issue, let us consider the linearisation.

14.
Linearisation

Let
M(v)=(2\pi)^{-d/2}e^{-|v|^{2}/2}
. Linearising the Boltzmann equation around
M
consists in writing
f=M(1+\varepsilon\,h)
and letting
\varepsilon\to 0
, then
Q(f,f)\simeq\varepsilon{\mathcal{L}}_{B}h
, where

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{B}h

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{M}\bigl[Q(M,Mh)+Q(Mh,M)\bigr]

\displaystyle=\int\bigl[h(v^{\prime})+h(v^{\prime}_{*})-h(v)-h(v_{*})\bigr]\,M(v_{*})\,B(v-v_{*},\sigma)\,dv_{*}\,d\sigma.

Of course the linearised Boltzmann equation is
\partial_{t}h={\mathcal{L}}_{B}h
.
Without loss of generality I assume
\int h(v)\,dv=0
,
\int h(v)\,v\,dv=0
,
\int h(v)\,|v|^{2}\,dv=0
.
Then as
\varepsilon\to 0
,

\begin{cases}\displaystyle H(M(1+\varepsilon h))\simeq H(M)+\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2}\|h\|_{L^{2}(M)}^{2},\\
\displaystyle I(M(1+\varepsilon h))\simeq I(M)+\varepsilon^{2}\|\nabla h\|_{L^{2}(M)}^{2},\\
\displaystyle\Gamma_{1}((\log(1+\varepsilon M))\simeq\varepsilon^{2}\Gamma_{1}(h).\end{cases}

So Theorem
13.1
admits the following linearised version involving the Dirichlet form

{\mathcal{I}}(h)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\nabla h(v)|^{2}\,M(v)\,dv.

Theorem 14.1

(Decay of the Dirichlet form along linearised Boltzmann)
.

With the same notation as in Theorem
13.1
, for
{\mathcal{I}}
to be nonincreasing along either the spatially homogeneous linearised Boltzmann or linearised Landau equation, it is sufficient that
\overline{\gamma}\leq\sqrt{4P_{*}}
, where
P_{*}
is the best constant in the following inequality: for all even
h:\mathbb{S}^{d-1}/\{\pm I\}\to\mathbb{R}
with
\int h\,dv=0
,

\bullet
For Boltzmann:

\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\Gamma_{\beta}(h)\leq\frac{1}{P_{*}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\Gamma_{1,\beta}(h).

\bullet
For Landau:

\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\Gamma_{1}(h)\leq\frac{1}{P_{*}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\Gamma_{2}(h).

Remark 14.2
.

The range of
\gamma
may possibly be further improved by restricting to functions
h
satisfying not only
\int h=0
, but also
\int h\,v\,dv=0
and
\int h|v|^{2}\,dv=0
.

The constant
P_{*}
a priori depends on whether we are considering Boltzmann or Landau, and a priori depends on
\beta
. For the Landau case, it cannot be larger than
\lambda_{1}
, which is thus the best uniform lower bound we may hope for. As we shall see later, that best possible bound does hold true:
P_{*}\geq\lambda_{1}
, and in general there is equality. Before that we need some reminders.

Bibliographical Notes

The relation between entropic inequalities and Poincaré (spectral gap) inequalities is very well known, see e.g.
[
16
,
188
]
. In the special case
\gamma=0
(Maxwellian molecules), the monotonicity of
{\mathcal{I}}
follows from the commutation property with
\Delta
; apart from that special case the general result of Theorem
14.1
is new as far as I know.

15.
Reminders on diffusion processes

Some reminders from the theory of diffusion of processes. Since Bakry and Émery, the theory has been built upon the concept of
curvature-dimension condition

{\rm CD}(K,N)
: Ricci curvature bounded below by
K
, dimension bounded above by
N
. Expressed in terms of the Laplace–Beltrami operator, this condition, set on a Riemannian manifold
(M,g)
, reads

\forall f:M\to\mathbb{R},\qquad\Gamma_{2}(f)\geq\frac{(\Delta f)^{2}}{N}+K\Gamma_{1}(f).

(Here
\Gamma_{1}(f)=\Gamma_{1}(f,f)
, etc.)
For instance the sphere satifies
{\rm CD}(d-2,d-1)
:

(15.1)

\forall f:\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\to\mathbb{R},\qquad\Gamma_{2}(f)\geq\frac{(\Delta f)^{2}}{d-1}+(d-2)\,\Gamma_{1}(f).

Keeping only the latter term, this obviously yields

(15.2)

\int f\Gamma_{2}(\log f)\geq(d-2)\int f\Gamma_{1}(\log f)=(d-2)I(f).

Along the heat flow,
dH/dt=-I
,
dI/dt=-2\int f\Gamma_{2}(\log f)
; so (
15.2
) is the same as

-\frac{d}{dt}I(e^{t\Delta}f)\geq-2(d-2)\frac{d}{dt}H(e^{t\Delta}f)\geq 0.

Upon integrating from
0\to\infty

à la
Stam, we discover

(15.3)

H(f)\leq\frac{1}{2(d-2)}I(f),

that is, the log Sobolev inequality with constant
d-2
. This is good, but not optimal. On the one hand, in dimension 2 the unit sphere
\mathbb{S}^{1}
has no curvature, but still satisfies log Sobolev, with constant 1. On the other hand, linearisation of (
15.3
) yields a Poincaré inequality with constant
d-2
, but we know from the discussion of spherical harmonics that the spectral gap for
-\Delta
on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
is
d-1
, not
d-2
. It turns out that
d-1
is indeed optimal, not only for Poincaré but also for log Sobolev: For all probability densities
f
on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, for all functions
h
on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
with
\int h=0
,

(15.4)

\int h^{2}\leq\frac{1}{d-1}\int\Gamma_{1}(h),

(15.5)

H(f)\leq\frac{1}{2(d-1)}I(f),

(15.6)

\int f\,\Gamma_{1}(\log f)\leq\frac{1}{d-1}\int f\,\Gamma_{2}(\log f).

To summarise: There are three functional inequalities of interest for us:

\bullet
Optimal Poincaré (spectral gap): For all functions
h
,

(15.7)

\int_{M}h^{2}-\left(\int_{M}h\right)^{2}\leq\frac{1}{\lambda_{1}(M)}\int_{M}|\nabla h|^{2}

\bullet
Optimal log Sobolev inequality: For all probability densities
f
,

(15.8)

\int_{M}f\log f\leq\frac{1}{2L(M)}\int_{M}f|\nabla\log f|^{2}

\bullet
Optimal differential log Sobolev inequality, or optimal differential Bakry–Émery inequality: For all probability densities
f
,

(15.9)

\int_{M}f|\nabla\log f|^{2}\leq\frac{1}{L_{*}(M)}\int f\Gamma_{2}(\log f);

and always

L_{*}\leq L\leq\lambda_{1},

while

L_{*}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})=L(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})=\lambda_{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})=d-1.

When this is applied to Theorem
13.1
and Theorem
14.1
, taking into account
\int\Gamma_{1}(\sqrt{F})=(1/4)I(F)
, this immediately yields a range of exponents for decay of the Fisher information along the Landau equation:
K_{*}=4L_{*}=4(d-1)
,
P_{*}=\lambda_{1}=d-1
, and in both cases
\overline{\gamma}\leq 2\sqrt{d-1}
. This is already good, and better than the crude bounds in Theorem
9.4
. But it still fails to cover the most important case, physically speaking,
\gamma=-3
in dimension
d=3
, in fact with this bound
\overline{\gamma}
can only go up to
2\sqrt{2}
, which is
<3
(not by much, but that is life!).

However there is a crucial information which was not yet exploited: Since
F
is assumed even in the criteria of Theorems
13.1
and
14.1
, this can only improve the constants; in fact we are looking for optimal constants on
\mathbb{R}\!\mathbb{P}^{d-1}
, rather than
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
.
For the spectral gap, we already know the answer: considering even functions amounts to rule out spaces
H_{\ell}
with odd
\ell
, so

(15.10)

\lambda_{1}(\mathbb{R}\!\mathbb{P}^{d-1})=\lambda_{2}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})=2d

(much better than
d-1
). Also, for
d=2
one can work out completely

L_{*}(\mathbb{R}\!\mathbb{P}^{1})=L(\mathbb{R}\!\mathbb{P}^{1})=4\quad(=\lambda_{1}(\mathbb{R}\!\mathbb{P}^{1})).

For higher dimensions the constants
L
and
L_{*}
are not as good: In fact

d\geq 3\Longrightarrow\qquad L_{*}(\mathbb{R}\!\mathbb{P}^{d-1})\leq L(\mathbb{R}\!\mathbb{P}^{d-1})<2d.

However, the excellent spectral gap already implies at least an improved bound on
L
: Indeed, a theorem by Rothaus shows that if
M
satisfies
{\rm CD}(K,N)
and
\lambda_{1}>KN/(N-1)
then
L(M)\geq[K(1-1/N)+(4/N)\lambda_{1}]/(1+1/N)^{2}
; in our case this yields

(15.11)

L(\mathbb{R}\!\mathbb{P}^{d-1})\geq(d-1)\left(\frac{d+2}{d}\right)^{2}.

(Note that it does capture the optimal constant
4
when
d=2
.) In dimension 3 it yields an excellent bound
60/9\simeq 5.55
for
L
. While this gives no lower bound for
L_{*}
, it suggests at least that the latter is also improved. In fact, one can establish

(15.12)

5.5\leq L_{*}(\mathbb{R}\!\mathbb{P}^{2})<5.74.

In higher dimensions, the bound gets worse and worse, but still one has at least that
2d=\lambda_{1}(\mathbb{R}\!\mathbb{P}^{d-1})\geq L_{*}(\mathbb{R}\!\mathbb{P}^{d-1})\geq L_{*}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})=d-1
.

Now we are ready to rework Theorems
13.1
and
14.1
, and obtain

Theorem 15.1

(Guillen–Silvestre theorem, full version)
.

Assume that the function
\Psi
appearing in Landau’s collision operator satisfies
\overline{\gamma}=\sup|r\Psi^{\prime}(r)/\Psi(r)-2|\leq 4
. Then

(i) Fisher’s information can only go down with
t
along the spatially homogeneous Landau equation;

(ii) The Dirichlet form
\int|\nabla h|^{2}\,M
can only go down with
t
along the spatially homogeneous linearised Landau equation.

Remark 15.2
.

The choice of the constant 4 in Theorem
15.1
is because the Landau equation theory assumes at least
\gamma\geq-4
.

Proof of Theorem
15.1
.

For (i): In view of Theorem
13.1
, suffices to check
L_{*}(\mathbb{R}\!\mathbb{P}^{d-1})\geq 4
. This is postponed to Section
20
: There I shall prove that
L_{*}(\mathbb{R}\!\mathbb{P}^{1})=4
,
L_{*}(\mathbb{R}\!\mathbb{P}^{2})\geq 4.5
(a bit short of the better bounds
4.75
and
5.5
obtained respectively by Guillen–Silvestre and by Ji, but quite sufficient for this theorem), and for higher dimensions
L_{*}(\mathbb{R}\!\mathbb{P}^{d-1})\geq 6d/(d+1)
(quite bad in large dimension since actually
L_{*}\geq d-1
, but also quite sufficient for this theorem).

Then, (ii) is simpler: It follows directly from (
15.10
) and Theorem
14.1
; actually any
\overline{\gamma}\leq\sqrt{8d}
will do.
∎

Now what about the Boltzmann equation, that is, what to do of the inequalities appearing in the first half of Theorems
13.1
and
14.1
? These ones do not fall within the range of usual functional inequalities, but they are related as the preceding chapters demonstrate. The next chapter will be devoted to the linearised case: a spectral gap problem. The subsequent four chapters will consider the nonlinear inequality.

Bibliographical Notes

There are two lines of thought behind curvature-dimension bounds: operators and geodesics. The first one goes via functional inequalities involving
\Delta
, as in the works of Bakry, Ledoux and others. The other one involves the behaviour of geodesics and the way they distort measure. This dichotomy can be seen as an Eulerian/Lagrangian duality; see
[
188
, Chapter 14]
. The second approach has been at the core of the synthetic theory of curvature-dimension bounds
[
188
, Part III]
. In the smooth setting, the Bochner formula relates the two approaches, which are therefore equivalent in a smooth context.

For functional inequalities in log Sobolev style, Bakry–Gentil–Ledoux
[
16
]
is a standard rich introduction to the subject; the differential Bakry–Émery inequality with optimal constants is Eq. (5.7.5) in that book. Another excellent introduction is the collective work
[
6
]
, in french.

Inequality (
15.4
) for
{\rm CD}(d-2,d-1)
manifolds is due to Lichérowicz, while (
15.5
) and (
15.6
) are part of the Bakry–Émery theory. The optimal inequality (
15.5
) is discussed in Ledoux
[
118
]
, who develops a unified approach to Poincaré and log Sobolev inequalities.

Michel Ledoux has kindly provided references on the problem of constants on
\mathbb{R}\!\mathbb{P}^{d-1}
, with the help of Fabrice Baudoin and Dominique Bakry. The theorem by Rothaus is from
[
154
]
. Saloff-Coste
[
156
]
considers the log Sobolev and Poincaré constants on
\mathbb{R}\!\mathbb{P}^{d-1}
and notices that the log Sobolev and Poincaré inequality differ (Theorem 9.2 therein), and that the ratio should go down to
1/2
as
d\to\infty
(Remark 3), so that in large dimension the sphere and projective space would have nearly similar
L
and
L_{*}
constants. Details do not seem to have been written down before. A lecture by Fontenas
[
80
]
addresses this and related topics. Sehyun Ji worked on this issue and presented preliminary results in the summer school Mathemata (July 2024), then announced
[
113
]
a lower bound
L_{*}(\mathbb{R}\!\mathbb{P}^{d-1})\geq d+3-1/(d-1)
.

16.
Spectral gap

This section addresses the problem of monotonicity for the Dirichlet form
{\mathcal{I}}
along the spatially homogeneous linearised Boltzmann equation; that is, the linearised version of the initial quest.

Consider the following commutative diagram, where
h
is a function on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
with zero average.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}h^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\Gamma_{\beta}(h)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\Gamma_{1}(h)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\displaystyle 2\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\Gamma_{1,\beta}(h)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{L_{\beta}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{\Delta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{\Delta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{\Delta}}

Knowing that
-\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{\Delta}\int h^{2}\geq\lambda_{1}\int\Gamma_{1}(h)
does not imply in itself that
\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{L_{\beta}}\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{\Delta}\frac{1}{2}\int h^{2}\geq-\lambda_{1}\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{L_{\beta}}\int\Gamma_{1}(h)
; it is actually the other way round. But these are symmetric operators with a common basis of eigenfunctions (spherical harmonics
e_{k}
), so we may write
(\lambda_{k})
and
(\nu_{k})
for the eigenvalues of
-\Delta
and
-L_{\beta}
, with repetition, and if
h=\sum x_{k}\,e_{k}
, assuming
\int h=0
,

\frac{1}{2}\int h^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k\geq 1}x_{k}^{2},\qquad\int\Gamma_{1}(h)=\sum_{k\geq 1}\lambda_{k}x_{k}^{2}

\int\Gamma_{\beta}(h)=\sum_{k\geq 1}\nu_{k}x_{k}^{2}\qquad 2\int\Gamma_{1,\beta}(h)=2\sum_{k\geq 1}\lambda_{k}\nu_{k}x_{k}^{2}.

Then

2\int\Gamma_{1,\beta}(h)\geq 2\lambda_{1}\sum_{k\geq 1}\nu_{k}x_{k}^{2}=2\lambda_{1}\int\Gamma_{\beta}(h).

Let
P_{*}
be as in Theorem
14.1
(first forget about the parity requirement); then the above computation shows that
P_{*}\geq\lambda_{1}
, whatever
\beta
, and also in the AGC. It is also immediate from those formulae that
\lambda_{1}
is achieved for
x=e_{1}
, except if
\nu_{1}=0
(which could arise from a peculiar choice of
\beta
; recall formula (
12.4
)). When only even functions are considered, as in the statement of Theorem
14.1
, the discussion is the same with
\lambda_{1}
and
\nu_{1}
replaced by
\lambda_{2}
and
\nu_{2}
.

The above discussion is summarised into the

Proposition 16.1
.

Whatever the collision kernel
\beta
, it holds
P_{*}\geq\lambda_{1}(\mathbb{R}\!\mathbb{P}^{d-1})=\lambda_{2}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})
, with equality as soon as
\nu_{2}(\beta)\neq 0
.

Remark 16.2
.

It is enlightening to work out directly the one-dimensional case. When
d=2
, identify
\mathbb{S}^{1}
with
\mathbb{R}/(2\pi\mathbb{Z})
, write
\dot{g}
for the usual derivative of
g
, then the problem becomes

(16.1)

\iint\bigl(g(\alpha^{\prime})-g(\alpha)\bigr)^{2}\,\beta(\cos(\alpha-\alpha^{\prime}))\,d\alpha\,d\alpha^{\prime}\leq\frac{1}{P_{*}}\iint\bigl(\dot{g}(\alpha^{\prime})-\dot{g}(\alpha)\bigr)^{2}\,\beta(\cos(\alpha-\alpha^{\prime}))\,d\alpha\,d\alpha^{\prime}.

Let us prove (
16.1
) by elementary means. Upon first sight, the optimal constant
P_{*}
will depend on
\beta
. But rewrite everything in Fourier series. Writing by abuse of notation
\mathaccent 866{\beta}
for
\mathaccent 1371{\beta\circ\cos}
,

\beta(\alpha)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathaccent 866{\beta}(k)\,\cos(k\alpha),\qquad g(\alpha)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathaccent 866{g}(k)\,e^{ik\alpha},

where

g(\alpha)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}g(t)\,e^{-i\,kt}\,dt.

By approximation we may assume that
\beta
is integrable. Then, using standard formulas of Fourier transform,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\iint

\displaystyle\bigl(g(\alpha^{\prime})-g(\alpha)\bigr)^{2}\,\beta(\cos(\alpha^{\prime}-\alpha))\,d\alpha\,d\alpha^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\int g(\alpha)^{2}\,d\alpha\int\beta(\cos\alpha^{\prime}))\,d\alpha^{\prime\prime}-2\int g(\alpha)\,g(\alpha^{\prime})\,\beta(\cos(\alpha^{\prime}-\alpha))\,d\alpha\,d\alpha^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\|g\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\left(\int\beta\right)-\int g(g\ast\beta)

\displaystyle=2\pi^{2}\Bigl[\bigl(\sum_{k}|\mathaccent 866{g}(k)|^{2}\bigr)\mathaccent 866{\beta}(0)-\Re\sum_{k}\mathaccent 866{g}(k)(\mathaccent 866{g}(k)\mathaccent 866{\beta}(k))^{*}\Bigr]

\displaystyle=2\pi^{2}\Bigl[\sum_{k}|\mathaccent 866{g}(k)|^{2}\bigl(\mathaccent 866{\beta}(0)-\Re\mathaccent 866{\beta}(k)\bigr)\Bigr]

\displaystyle=2\pi^{2}\Bigl[\sum_{k}|\mathaccent 866{g}(k)|^{2}\bigl(\mathaccent 866{\beta}(0)-\mathaccent 866{\beta}(k)\bigr)\Bigr].

So (
16.1
) is equivalent to

(16.2)

\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}|\mathaccent 866{g}(k)|^{2}\bigl(\mathaccent 866{\beta}(0)-\mathaccent 866{\beta}(k)\bigr)\leq\frac{1}{P_{*}}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}|k|^{2}|\mathaccent 866{g}(k)|^{2}\bigl(\mathaccent 866{\beta}(0)-\mathaccent 866{\beta}(k)\bigr).

The coefficients
\mathaccent 866{\beta}(0)-\mathaccent 866{\beta}(k)
are nonnegative since
\beta
is, and vanish for
k=0
. So (
16.2
) holds with
P_{*}=1
. Now if we recall the additional condition that
g
is even, i.e.
g(\alpha+\pi)=g(\alpha)
, only even coefficients
k
remain, and
\mathaccent 866{g}(\pm 1)=0
, so the constant
P_{*}
improves from 1 to
4
. This handles the one-dimensional case. ∎

The Boltzmann analogue of Theorem
15.1
(ii) now comes as an easy consequence of the previous estimates:

Theorem 16.3

(Decay of the Dirichlet form along linearised Boltzmann)
.

With the notation (
10.4
), let
B=B(|v-v_{*}|,\cos\theta)
be a Boltzmann kernel satisfying
\overline{\gamma}(B)\leq 4
. Then the Dirichlet form
\int|\nabla h|^{2}M
can only go down with
t
along the spatially homogeneous linearised Boltzmann equation.

Proof of Theorem
16.3
.

Combining Theorem
14.1
with Proposition
16.1
yields the decay for
\overline{\gamma}(B)\leq\sqrt{4\lambda_{2}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}=\sqrt{8d}
, which is always at least 4.
∎

17.
Local monotonicity criterion via curvature

Now we are back to the study of Criterion
13.1
for the nonlinear Boltzmann equation.
Recall the expression of
\Gamma_{\beta}
and
\Gamma_{1,\beta}
from (
11.4
)–(
11.5
). By analogy with the diffusive case, we may call inequality (
13.1
) a
\beta
-nonlocal differential log Sobolev inequality. As in Section
9
, let us separate
\Gamma_{1,\beta}
into two terms, one involving variations of the gradient and the other being “curvature-like”, pointwise in the gradient:

(17.1)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{1,\beta}(f)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int\bigl|\nabla f(\sigma)-P_{k\sigma}\nabla f(k)\bigr|^{2}\,\beta(k\cdot\sigma)\,d\sigma+\frac{1}{2}\int\bigl(|\nabla f(k)|^{2}-|P_{k\sigma}\nabla f(k)|^{2}\bigr)\,\beta(k\cdot\sigma)\,d\sigma

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int\bigl|\nabla f(\sigma)-P_{k\sigma}\nabla f(k)\bigr|^{2}\,\beta(k\cdot\sigma)\,d\sigma+(d-2)\,\Sigma(\beta)\,|\nabla f(k)|^{2},

where I used the second identity in Proposition
8.12
(x) and Lemma
9.1
, and

(17.2)

\Sigma(\beta)=\frac{1}{2(d-1)}\int\bigl[1-(k\cdot\sigma)^{2}\bigr]\,\beta(k\cdot\sigma)\,d\sigma.

In particular,

\Gamma_{1,\beta}(f)\geq(d-2)\,\Sigma(\beta)\,|\nabla f|^{2}.

The first lowest hanging fruit from Criterion
13.1
is to rely on just the curvature term:

(17.3)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}F\,\Gamma_{1,\beta}(\log F)

\displaystyle\geq(d-2)\,\Sigma(\beta)\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}F(k)|\nabla\log F(k)|^{2}\,dk

\displaystyle=4(d-2)\,\Sigma(\beta)\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}|\nabla\sqrt{F}|^{2}.

Remarkably,
\Sigma
turns out the be
exactly
the optimal constant relating
\int\Gamma_{\beta}(g)
and
\int\Gamma_{1}(g)
.

Proposition 17.1
.

For any even function
g:\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\to\mathbb{R}
,

(17.4)

\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\Gamma_{\beta}(g)\leq\Sigma(\beta)\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\Gamma_{1}(g),

and the constant
\Sigma(\beta)
is optimal (lowest).

Before proving Proposition
17.1
, I will state some striking consequences. First, combined with (
17.3
), Proposition
17.1
implies a universal (independent of
\beta
) bound:

Corollary 17.2

(Curvature-based nonlocal differential log Sobolev)
.

For any even function
F:\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
, for any
\beta:[-1,1]\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
with
\int\theta^{2}\beta(\cos\theta)\sin^{d-2}\theta\,d\theta<\infty
,

(17.5)

\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\Gamma_{1,\beta}(\log F)\geq 4(d-2)\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\Gamma_{\beta}(\sqrt{F}).

A very simple decay criterion follows:

Theorem 17.3

(Curvature-induced decay of
I
for the Boltzmann equation)
.

If the collision kernel
B=B(|v-v_{*}|,\cos\theta)
satisfies

\sup_{r>0,\ 0\leq\theta\leq\pi}\left\{\frac{r}{B}\left|\frac{\partial B}{\partial r}\right|(r,\cos\theta)\right\}\leq 2\sqrt{d-2},

then
I
is nonincreasing along solutions of the spatially homogeneous Boltzmann equation.

That covers for instance
|\gamma|\leq 2
in any dimension
d\geq 3
.

Proof of Theorem
17.3
.

As in Corollary
17.2
, for any section
\beta_{r}(\cos\theta)=B(r,\cos\theta)
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}F\Gamma_{1,\beta_{r}}(\log F)

\displaystyle\geq(d-2)\Sigma(\beta_{r})\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}F|\nabla\log F|^{2}

\displaystyle=4(d-2)\,\Sigma(\beta_{r})\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\Gamma_{1}(\sqrt{F})

\displaystyle\geq 4(d-2)\,\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\Gamma_{\beta_{r}}(\sqrt{F}),

and Theorem
13.1
(or actually the slightly generalised version coming from Theorem
10.2
when
B
is not necessarily in product form and (
10.4
) is used) applies with
\overline{\gamma}(B,r)=\sqrt{4(d-2)}
.
∎

It remains to prove Proposition
17.1
. I shall provide two arguments. The first one carries some intuition of Poincaré inequalities but fails to achieve the sharp bound. The second one carries no intuition at all, but provides the sharp bound.

Proof of Proposition
17.1
, nonsharp bound.

Here I will prove

(17.6)

\frac{1}{2}\iint\bigl[g(\sigma)-g(k)\bigr]^{2}\,\beta(k\cdot\sigma)\,dk\,d\sigma\\
\leq\frac{1}{2(d-1)}\left(|\mathbb{S}^{d-2}|\int_{0}^{\pi}\theta^{2}\beta(\cos\theta)\,\sin^{d-2}\theta\,d\theta\right)\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}|\nabla g|^{2}.

Replacing
\beta
by
[\beta(\cos\theta)+\beta(-\cos\theta)]1_{\cos\theta\geq 0}
does not change the integral since
g(-\sigma)=g(\sigma)
. So we may assume that
\beta
is supported in
\theta\in[0,\pi/2]
. Then (
17.6
) differs from (
17.4
) by a factor at most
\sup(\theta^{2}/\sin^{2}\theta)=\pi^{2}/4
.

By linearity it is enough to prove (
17.6
) when
\beta
is concentrated on some fixed angle
\theta
. Then the estimate becomes

\iint\bigl[g(\sigma)-g(k)\bigr]^{2}\,1_{k\cdot\sigma=\cos\theta}\,dk\,d\sigma\leq\frac{\theta^{2}}{d-1}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}|\nabla g|^{2}.

Given
k
, each
\sigma
such that
k\cdot\sigma=\cos\theta
is the endpoint of a geodesic
[0,\theta]\to\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
coming from
k
with initial velocity
\phi\in S^{d-2}_{\bot}
, the
(d-2)
orthonormal sphere centered at
k
and orthogonal to it. In other words,
\phi
lies in the unitary tangent space to
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
at
k
. So the estimate to prove really is

\iint_{U\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\bigl[g(\exp_{k}\theta\phi)-g(k)\bigr]^{2}\,d\phi\,d\sigma\leq\frac{\theta^{2}}{d-1}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}|\nabla g|^{2},

where
U\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
is the unitary tangent bundle.
For each
k
and
\phi
we write
\gamma_{k,\phi}(t)=\exp_{k}(t\phi)
, and

g(\exp_{k}(\theta\phi))-g(k)=\int_{0}^{\theta}\nabla g(\gamma_{k,\phi}(t))\cdot\dot{\gamma}_{k,\phi}(t)\,dt

so

\Bigl(g(\exp_{k}(\theta\phi))-f(k)\Bigr)^{2}=\left(\int_{0}^{\theta}\nabla g(\gamma_{k,\phi}(t))\cdot\dot{\gamma}_{k,\phi}(t)\,dt\right)^{2}\leq\theta\int_{0}^{\theta}\bigl(\nabla g(\gamma_{k,\phi}(t))\cdot\dot{\gamma}_{k,\phi}(t)\bigr)^{2}\,dt.

Upon integration and using the fact that for each
t
the exponential map is measure preserving from the unitary tangent bundle to itself,

\displaystyle\iint_{U\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\Bigl(g(\exp_{k}(\theta\phi))-g(k)\Bigr)^{2}\,dk\,d\phi

\displaystyle\leq\theta\int_{0}^{\theta}\iint_{U\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\bigl(\nabla g(\gamma_{k,\phi}(t))\cdot\dot{\gamma}_{k,\phi}(t)\bigr)^{2}\,dt\,dk\,d\phi

\displaystyle=\theta\int_{0}^{\theta}\iint_{U\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\bigl(\nabla g(x)\cdot v\bigr)^{2}\,dt\,dx\,dv

\displaystyle=\theta^{2}\iint_{U\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\bigl(\nabla g(x)\cdot v\bigr)^{2}\,dx\,dv

\displaystyle=\frac{\theta^{2}}{d-1}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}|\nabla g(x)|^{2}\,dx.

∎

Proof of Proposition
17.1
, sharp bound.

Now the goal is

(17.7)

\frac{1}{2}\iint\bigl[g(\sigma)-g(k)\bigr]^{2}\,\beta(k\cdot\sigma)\,dk\,d\sigma\\
\leq\frac{1}{2(d-1)}\left(|\mathbb{S}^{d-2}|\int_{0}^{\pi}\sin^{2}\theta\,\beta(\cos\theta)\,\sin^{d-2}\theta\,d\theta\right)\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}|\nabla g|^{2}.

In other words,

\langle-L_{\beta}g,g\rangle\leq\Sigma(\beta)\,\langle-\Delta g,g\rangle.

It suffices to check this for all even spherical harmonics; in other words, that for all
\ell\in 2\mathbb{N}
,

\nu_{\ell}(\beta)\leq\Sigma(\beta)\,\lambda_{\ell}^{(d)},

where
\nu_{\ell}(\beta)
is the eigenvalue of
-L_{\beta}
in
H_{\ell}
, and
\lambda_{\ell}^{(d)}(\beta)
the one of
-\Delta
. This is the same as

\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\bigl[1-P_{\ell}(k\cdot\sigma)\bigr]\,\beta(k\cdot\sigma)\,d\sigma\leq\frac{\ell(\ell+d-2)}{2(d-1)}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\bigl[1-(k\cdot\sigma)^{2}\bigr]\,\beta(k\cdot\sigma)\,d\sigma.

The proof is concluded by Lemma
17.4
below.
∎

Lemma 17.4
.

For all
d\in\mathbb{N}
, for all
\ell\in 2\mathbb{N}
,

(17.8)

\sup_{-1\leq x\leq 1}\left(\frac{1-P_{\ell}^{(d)}(x)}{1-x^{2}}\right)\leq\frac{\ell(\ell+d-2)}{2(d-1)},

with equality for
\ell=2
(for all
x
) and for
x\to\pm 1
(for all
\ell
).

Proof of Lemma
17.4
.

Throughout the proof, superscripts
(d)
are implicit. First some reformulations: Since
\lambda_{\ell}=\ell(\ell+d-2)
and
P_{2}(x)=(dx^{2}-1)/(d-1)
, the inequality amounts to

\text{ $\ell$ even }\Longrightarrow\forall x,\quad\frac{1-P_{\ell}(x)}{\lambda_{\ell}}\leq\frac{1-P_{2}(x)}{\lambda_{2}}.

Since
P_{\ell}(\pm 1)=1
and
P^{\prime}_{\ell}(\pm 1)=\pm\lambda_{\ell}
from (
12.1
), the inequality also says that
P_{\ell}
remains above the parabola which is tangent to its graph at
x=\pm 1
.

Now for the proof of the Lemma itself. For pedagogical reasons, first consider
d=2
. Then
\lambda_{2}=4
,
\lambda_{2n}=4n^{2}
, and from the interpretation of
P_{\ell}
(Example
12.2
), (
17.8
) is the same as

\forall n\in\mathbb{N},\qquad\frac{1-\cos(2n\alpha)}{1-\cos^{2}\alpha}\leq 2n^{2}.

Equivalently, since
1-\cos(2n\alpha)=2\sin^{2}(n\alpha)
and
1-\cos^{2}\alpha=\sin^{2}\alpha
, the inequality amounts to just

(17.9)

|\sin(n\alpha)|\leq n\,\sin\alpha,

which is geometrically obvious and can also be proven by induction using
|\sin(n\alpha+\alpha)|\leq|\cos(n\alpha)|\sin\alpha+|\sin(n\alpha)||\cos\alpha|\leq\sin\alpha+|\sin(n\alpha)|
.

To handle the general case, recall that Formula
12.3
reduces the general case to the 2-dimensional situation. In view of that formula and Remark
12.3
,

(17.10)

1-P_{\ell}(x)=\frac{|\mathbb{S}^{d-3}|}{|\mathbb{S}^{d-2}|}\int_{-1}^{1}\bigl[1-(x+is\sqrt{1-x^{2}})^{\ell}\bigr]\,(1-s^{2})^{\frac{d-4}{2}}\,ds.

We turn to estimate the real part of the integrand (the imaginary part vanishes by symmetry).
Given
s
and
x
both
[-1,1]
, let
\rho=\sqrt{x^{2}+s^{2}(1-x^{2})}
,

\cos\alpha=x/\rho
,
\sin\alpha=s\sqrt{1-x^{2}}/\rho
, so that
x+is\sqrt{1-x^{2}}=\rho e^{i\alpha}
.

Claim:

(17.11)

\forall n\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\ \rho\in[0,1],\ \alpha\in[0,\pi]\qquad\Re\bigl[1-(\rho e^{i\alpha})^{2n}\bigr]\leq n(1-\rho^{2})+2n^{2}\rho^{2}\sin^{2}\alpha.

The proof of (
17.11
) is by induction again. For
n=0
the identity is obvious. If true at step
n
, then

\displaystyle\Re\bigl[1-(\rho e^{i\alpha})^{2n+2}\bigr]

\displaystyle=\Re\bigl[1-(\rho e^{i\alpha})^{2n}\bigr]+\Re\bigl[(\rho e^{i\alpha})^{2n}-(\rho e^{i\alpha})^{2n+2)}\bigr]

\displaystyle=\Re\bigl[1-(\rho e^{i\alpha})^{2n}\bigr]+\rho^{2n}(1-\rho^{2})\cos(2n\alpha)+\rho^{2n+2}[\cos 2n\alpha-\cos(2n+2)\alpha]

\displaystyle=\Re\bigl[1-(\rho e^{i\alpha})^{2n}\bigr]+\rho^{2n}(1-\rho^{2})\cos(2n\alpha)-2\rho^{2n+2}\sin(2n+1)\alpha\,\sin\alpha.

Using both the induction hypothesis and the bound (
17.9
), the above is bounded by

\bigl(n(1-\rho^{2})+2n^{2}\rho^{2}\sin^{2}\alpha\bigr)+(1-\rho^{2})+2(2n+1)\rho^{2}\sin^{2}\alpha\leq(n+1)(1-\rho^{2})+2(n+1)^{2}\rho^{2}\sin^{2}\alpha,

which propagates the induction hypothesis and proves (
17.11
). So

\displaystyle\Re\bigl[1-(x+is\sqrt{1-x^{2}})^{2n}\bigr]

\displaystyle\leq\bigl[n(1-s)^{2}+2n^{2}s^{2}\bigr]\,(1-x^{2})

\displaystyle\leq\bigl[n(1-2n)(1-s^{2})+2n^{2}\bigr](1-x^{2}).

Plugging this back in (
17.10
), we get

\displaystyle\frac{1-P_{\ell}(x)}{1-x^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{|\mathbb{S}^{d-3}|}{|\mathbb{S}^{d-2}|}\int_{-1}^{1}\bigl[n(1-2n)(1-s^{2})^{\frac{d-2}{2}}+2n^{2}(1-s^{2})^{\frac{d-4}{2}}\bigr]\,ds

\displaystyle=\frac{|\mathbb{S}^{d-3}|}{|\mathbb{S}^{d-2}|}\left(n(1-2n)\frac{|\mathbb{S}^{d}|}{|\mathbb{S}^{d-2}|}+2n^{2}\frac{|\mathbb{S}^{d-2}|}{|\mathbb{S}^{d-3}|}\right)

\displaystyle=n(1-2n)\frac{|\mathbb{S}^{d-3}|\,|\mathbb{S}^{d}|}{|\mathbb{S}^{d-2}|\,|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|}+2n^{2}

\displaystyle=n(1-2n)\left(\frac{\int_{0}^{\pi}\sin^{d-1}\psi\,d\psi}{\int_{0}^{\pi}\sin^{d-3}\psi\,d\psi}\right)+2n^{2}

\displaystyle=n(1-2n)\left(\frac{d-2}{d-1}\right)+2n^{2}=\frac{2n(n+d-2)}{d-1},

as required, after application of the Wallis integrals again.
∎

Bibliographical Notes

Poincaré estimates in positive curvature are classical and appear e.g. in
[
188
, Chapter 18]
, but the setting here allows for much more explicit constants, as Proposition
17.1
shows. All this section (except the nonoptimal argument for Proposition
17.1
) is taken from
[
111
]
.

18.
(Counter)examples

Theorem
17.3
provides a general answer, even if nonoptimal, in dimension
d\geq 3
, but says nothing of dimension 2. This may seem awkward as dimension 2 is usually simpler for the theory of the Boltzmann equation, and as a matter of fact the monotonicity property of the Fisher information for Maxwellian kernels was established in dimension 2 several years before higher dimensions.

It turns out that this is in the order of things. The general criterion established in Section
10
fails for dimension 2, at least for certain classes of singular kernels. At the opposite side, when the collision kernel is constant in the angular variable (which is arguably the most regular that can be!) then the criterion holds even in dimension 2, and this will allow for a first positive result for non-Maxwell kernels in that dimension.

Theorem 18.1
.

(i) Consider a measure
\beta
on
[0,\pi]
defined by

\beta(d\theta)=\sum_{i=0}^{N}\beta_{i}\,\delta_{\theta_{i}},\quad\theta_{i}=\frac{i\pi}{N},\ N\in\mathbb{N},\ \beta_{i}\geq 0,\ \sum_{i=1}^{N-1}\beta_{i}>0.

Then

(18.1)

\inf\left\{\frac{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}F\,\Gamma_{1,\beta}(\log F)}{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\Gamma_{\beta}(\sqrt{F})};\quad F:\mathbb{S}^{1}/\{\pm I\}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}\right\}=0.

(ii) If
\beta
is constant on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, then for any
d\geq 2
,

(18.2)

\inf\left\{\frac{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}F\,\Gamma_{1,\beta}(\log F)}{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\Gamma_{\beta}(\sqrt{F})};\quad F:\mathbb{S}^{d-1}/\{\pm I\}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}\right\}\geq 4d.

(iii) For the symmetrised hard spheres kernel in dimension 2,

(18.3)

\inf\left\{\frac{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}F\,\Gamma_{1,\beta}(\log F)}{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\Gamma_{\beta}(\sqrt{F})};\quad F:\mathbb{S}^{1}/\{\pm I\}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}\right\}\geq 4\sqrt{2}.

In particular,
I
is nonincreasing along solutions of the 2-dimensional spatially homogeneous Boltzmann equation for hard spheres.

Remarks 18.2
.

1. In Part (i),
\delta_{0}
and
\delta_{\pi}
play no role in either
\Gamma_{\beta}
or
\Gamma_{1,\beta}
(
\delta_{0}
obviously corresponds to no deviation in the collision, and so does
\delta_{\pi}
when applied to
\pi
-periodic functions). So we may work directly with
\theta_{1},\ldots\theta_{N-1}
. By setting some coefficients
\beta_{i}
equal to 0, we see that the theorem covers any kernel with finite support in
\mathbb{Q}\pi
. Extension to angles which are irrational to
\pi
is a more difficult problem. Another question left open by this theorem is whether one can use this negative result to construct
f=f(v)
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that
I^{\prime}(f)\cdot Q(f,f)>0
, say for
B=|v-v_{*}|\,\beta
. Theorem
19.1
below will show that Part (i) would be false if
\beta
would not vanish.

(ii) Part (ii) implies that for
B(v-v_{*},\sigma)=|v-v_{*}|^{\gamma}
,
|\gamma|\leq 2\sqrt{d}
,
I
is nonincreasing along solutions of the spatially homogeneous Boltzmann equation. But only the case
\gamma=1
,
d=3
corresponds to a classical interaction considered in physics, namely three-dimensional hard spheres (already covered by Theorem
17.3
). Recall that in dimension 2 the hard spheres kernel vanishes for
\theta=0
and in dimensions 4 and higher it has an integrable singularity as
\theta\to 0
. Still (iii) tells us that hard spheres in dimension 2 can be handled, this will be done by using the obvious perturbation lemma below.

Lemma 18.3

(Perturbation lemma for the integral criterion)
.

If
\beta_{0}
and
\beta
are two collision kernels, and
K_{*}(\beta_{0})
,
K_{*}(\beta)
are the respective associated optimal constants in the criterion from Theorem
13.1
. If there are constants
m,M>0
such that

\forall\theta\in[0,\pi],\qquad m\,\beta_{0}(\cos\theta)\leq\beta(\cos\theta)\leq M\,\beta_{0}(\cos\theta),

Then

K_{*}(\beta)\geq\frac{m}{M}\,K_{*}(\beta_{0}).

Proof of Theorem
18.1
(i).

Fix
0<\delta<1/2
and in
\mathbb{R}/\pi\mathbb{Z}
let
I_{i}=[(i-\delta)\pi/N,(i+\delta)\pi/N]
,
1\leq i\leq N
, so that the intervals
I_{i}
are disjoint neighbourhoods of
\theta_{i}
. Pick up two smooth functions
\mathbb{R}/\pi\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
,
h
and
\psi
, such that

\bullet

h
is constant on each
I_{i}
and takes distinct integer values on all these intervals;

\bullet

\psi
is
\pi/N
-periodic, nonzero and supported over the intervals
I_{i}
.

Then for
A>0
let
F=(1+A\psi)h
. Since
\psi
is
\pi/N
-periodic, so is
\log(1+A\psi)^{\cdot}
, so

(\log F)^{\cdot}(\alpha+\theta_{i})-(\log F)^{\cdot}(\alpha)=(\log h)^{\cdot}(\alpha+\theta_{i})-(\log h)^{\cdot}(\alpha)

and thus

\int F\Gamma_{1,\beta}(\log F)=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i}\beta_{i}\int(1+A\psi(\alpha))h(\alpha)\bigl[(\log h)^{\cdot}(\alpha+\theta_{i})-(\log h)^{\cdot}(\alpha)\bigr]^{2}\,d\alpha.

On the support of
\psi
,
h
is constant, and so is
h(\cdot+\theta_{i})
; thus
\psi[(\log h)^{\cdot}(\alpha+\theta_{i})-(\log h)^{\cdot}(\alpha)]^{2}=0
. In the end,

\displaystyle\int F\Gamma_{1,\beta}(\log F)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i}\beta_{i}\int h(\alpha)\bigl[(\log h)^{\cdot}(\alpha+\theta_{i})-(\log h)^{\cdot}(\alpha)\bigr]^{2}\,d\alpha

\displaystyle=\int h\Gamma_{1,\beta}(h).

This is independent of
A
and
\psi
.

On the other hand,

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\beta}(\sqrt{F})

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i,j}\beta_{i}\int_{I_{j}}\bigl[\sqrt{F(\alpha+\theta_{i})}-\sqrt{F(\alpha)}\bigr]^{2}\,d\alpha

\displaystyle=A\left(\sum_{i}\beta_{i}\right)\left(\sqrt{\psi(I_{j})}-\sqrt{\psi(I_{i})}\right)^{2}\left(\sum_{j}\int_{I_{j}}h\right)^{2}.

∎

Proof of Theorem
18.1
(ii).

Take
\beta\equiv 1
. Then

\displaystyle\int F\Gamma_{1,\beta}(\log F)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\iint F(k)\bigl|\nabla\log F(\sigma)-\nabla\log F(k)\bigr|_{k,\sigma}^{2}\,dk\,d\sigma

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\iint F(k)|\nabla\log F(k)|^{2}\,dk\,d\sigma+\frac{1}{2}\iint F(k)|\nabla\log F(\sigma)|^{2}\,dk\,d\sigma

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad-\iint\nabla F(k)P_{\sigma k}\nabla\log F(\sigma)\,dk\,d\sigma

\displaystyle=\frac{|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|}{2}\int F|\nabla\log F|^{2}+\left(\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}F\right)\left(\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}|\nabla\sqrt{F}|^{2}\right)+0,

since
\int P_{\sigma k}\nabla\log F(\sigma)\,d\sigma=0
by Lemma
8.5
(recall
M_{\sigma k}\nabla f=P_{\sigma k}\nabla f
if
\nabla f(\sigma)\cdot\sigma=0
).
So

(18.4)

\int F\Gamma_{1,\beta}(\log F)\geq 2|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|\int|\nabla\sqrt{F}|^{2}.

On the other hand,

\displaystyle\int\Gamma_{\beta}(\sqrt{F})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\iint\bigl(\sqrt{F(\sigma)}-\sqrt{F(k)}\bigr)^{2}\,dk\,d\sigma

\displaystyle=|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|\int F-\left(\int\sqrt{F}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|^{2}\left[\frac{1}{|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|}\int F-\left(\frac{1}{|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|}\int\sqrt{F}\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|^{2}}{\lambda_{1}}\left(\frac{1}{|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}|\nabla\sqrt{F}|^{2}\right),

where
\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{1}(\mathbb{R}\!\mathbb{P}^{d-1})=2d
. The conclusion follows.
∎

Proof of Theorem
18.1
(iii).

For
d=2
the hard spheres kernel
b(\cos\theta)
varies like
\sin(\theta/2)
. Its symmetric version
[b(\cos\theta)+b(-\cos\theta)]/2
like
[\sin(\theta/2)+\cos(\theta/2)]/2
, which takes values in
[1/2,\sqrt{2}/2]
. So we may apply Lemma
18.3
with
\beta_{0}=1
and
M/m=\sqrt{2}
, leading to (
18.3
).
∎

Bibliographical Notes

Counterexample (i) was shown to me by Luis Silvestre (for
N=2
). Estimates (i)-(ii)-(iii) are published in
[
111
]
.

The perturbation lemma
18.3
is obvious, but it is natural, in this context of log Sobolev type inequalities, to link it to the Holley–Stroock perturbation lemma
[
106
]
.

19.
Local-integral monotonicity criterion via positivity

Combining the example of constant kernel in Section
18
, a comparison argument and the Poincaré inequality from Section
17
, is enough to show that
K_{*}>0
, thereby ruling out the situation of counterexample
18.1
(i) and proving the following monotonicity theorem.

Theorem 19.1

(Positivity-induced decay of
I
for the Boltzmann equation)
.

If the collision kernel
B=B(|v-v_{*}|,\cos\theta)
is such that for all
r>0
and (any)
k\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
,

\sup_{0\leq\theta\leq\pi}\left\{\frac{r}{B}\left|\frac{\partial B}{\partial r}\right|(r,\cos\theta)\right\}\leq\left(\frac{4(d-1)|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|\,\left(\displaystyle\min_{0\leq\theta\leq\pi}B(r,\cos\theta)\right)}{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\bigl[1-(k\cdot\sigma)^{2}\bigr]B(r,k\cdot\sigma)\,d\sigma}\right)^{1/2},

then
I
is nonincreasing along solutions of the spatially homogeneous Boltzmann equation. Such is the case if
B=|v-v_{*}|^{\gamma}\beta(\cos\theta)
with

(19.1)

|\gamma|\leq\left(\frac{4(d-1)|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|(\min\beta)}{|\mathbb{S}^{d-2}|\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\pi}\sin^{d}\theta\,\beta(\cos\theta)\,d\theta}\right)^{1/2}.

Proof of Theorem
19.1
.

Let
\beta_{r}(\cos\theta)=\beta(r,\cos\theta)
and
m_{r}=\min(\beta_{r})
. On the one hand, by (
18.4
),

\int F\Gamma_{1,\beta_{r}}(\log F)\geq\int F\Gamma_{1,m_{r}}(\log F)\geq 2|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|m_{r}\int|\nabla\sqrt{F}|^{2},

on the other hand, from Proposition
17.1
,

\int\Gamma_{\beta_{r}}(\sqrt{F})\leq\Sigma(\beta_{r})\int|\nabla\sqrt{F}|^{2}.

So for all even
F:\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
,

K_{*}(\beta_{r})\geq\frac{2|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|\,m_{r}}{\Sigma(\beta_{r})}

and the conclusion follows from Theorem
10.2
and the definitions of
m_{r},\Sigma(\beta_{r})
.
∎

While this approach provides a considerable list of kernels, even in dimension 2, for which the Fisher information is decaying, it is not powerful enough to handle all classical inverse-law potentials.

Example 19.2
.

Assume
d=2
and inverse power law forces like
1/r^{s}
. The collision kernel takes the form
B=|v-v_{*}|^{\gamma}b(\cos\theta)
with
\gamma=(s-3)/(s-1)
. Maxwellian molecules correspond to
s=3
. This kernel is bounded from below, so the right-hand side of (
19.1
) is a strictly positive number. As
s
varies,
\gamma=\gamma(s)
varies continuously, but also the right-hand side of (
19.1
). By continuity there is an interval around
s=3
in which the inequality (
19.1
) remains satisfied. Numerics can tell us exactly which is this range, but we already see that this method covers some of the classical inverse power law forces in dimension 2, which the curvature-based approach was unable to tackle.

Example 19.3
.

Let us now consider the case
s=2
, still in dimension
d=2
. Then
\gamma=-1
and
b(\cos\theta)
is proportional to
1/\sin^{2}(\theta/2)
. (This is the 2-dimensional version of Rutherford’s cross-section formula.) An explicit calculation shows that the right-hand side in (
19.1
) is
1/\sqrt{2}
, which is not enough to squeeze
|\gamma|=1
in the left-hand side. So Theorem
19.1
is not general enough to cover
s=2
in dimension
d=2
.

20.
Exploiting gradient variations I: the diffusive case

In this and the next two sections the question is how to exploit the variation of gradients in
\Gamma_{1,\beta}
, that is mainly the term
({\rm II})_{1}
in (
9.4
). In the diffusive case (
\nu=2
) this reduces to a Hessian term. Exploiting this Hessian is a classical topic: it corresponds to the difference between
{\rm CD}(K,\infty)
and
{\rm CD}(K,N)
bounds. It will be the subject of the present section. Unfortunately (or fortunately for later work), most if not all the recipes of the diffusive case will turn out difficult to adapt to the Boltzmann situation, as will be reviewed in Section
21
. However, in Section
22
a strategy will be presented, which is based on the reduction to the diffusive case and suffices to handle all the classical situations which are not covered by Theorem
17.3
.

20.1.
Dimension 2: The circle

Let us start with
d=2
, then there is no curvature, and Theorem
10.2
is about functions of one variable
\theta
in
\mathbb{S}^{1}
or
\mathbb{S}^{1}/\{\pm I\}
. In this diffusive context there is no risk of confusion with post-collisional velocities, so I shall use primes for regular derivatives in
\mathbb{R}
. The problem is the differential Bakry–Émery inequality

(20.1)

\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}[(\sqrt{f})^{\prime}]^{2}\leq\frac{1}{K_{*}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}f\bigl[(\log f)^{\prime\prime}\bigr]^{2}.

At this point there is a fascinating calculus combining nonlinearities and derivatives in dimension 1, which was discovered and rediscovered several times, in particular by McKean:

\displaystyle\int f[(\log f)^{\prime\prime}]^{2}

\displaystyle=\int f\left(\frac{f^{\prime\prime}}{f}-\frac{f^{\prime 2}}{f^{2}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\int\frac{(f^{\prime\prime})^{2}}{f}-2\int\frac{f^{\prime\prime}f^{\prime 2}}{f^{2}}+\int\frac{f^{\prime 4}}{f^{3}},

and by integration by parts

(20.2)

\int\frac{f^{\prime\prime}f^{\prime 2}}{f^{2}}=\frac{2}{3}\int\frac{f^{\prime 4}}{f^{3}}.

So that

(20.3)

\int f[(\log f)^{\prime\prime}]^{2}=\int\frac{f^{\prime\prime 2}}{f}-\frac{1}{3}\int\frac{f^{\prime 4}}{f^{3}},

an algebraic relation involving three quantities which all are natural “higher order” generalisations of the Fisher information.

Remark 20.1
.

Choosing
\alpha\in(0,1)
we find

\frac{1}{\alpha}\frac{((f^{\alpha})^{\prime\prime})^{2}}{f^{2\alpha-1}}=\frac{f^{\prime\prime 2}}{f}+(\alpha-1)^{2}\frac{f^{\prime 4}}{f^{3}}+2(\alpha-1)\frac{f^{\prime\prime}f^{\prime 2}}{f^{2}},

so by (
20.2
)

\frac{1}{\alpha}\int\frac{(f^{\alpha})^{\prime\prime 2}}{f^{2\alpha-1}}=\int\frac{f^{\prime\prime 2}}{ff}+\left[(\alpha-1)^{2}+\frac{4}{3}(\alpha-1)\right]\int\frac{f^{\prime 4}}{f^{3}}.

In particular, the nonnegativity of the left hand side implies

\int\frac{f^{\prime 4}}{f^{3}}\leq\frac{1}{(1-\alpha)(\alpha+\frac{1}{3})}\int\frac{f^{\prime\prime 2}}{f},

which is optimal for
\alpha=1/3
and yields

(20.4)

\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\frac{f^{\prime 4}}{f^{3}}\leq\frac{9}{4}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\frac{f^{\prime\prime 2}}{f}.

Plugging this back in (
20.3
) further yields

\int f[(\log f)^{\prime\prime}]^{2}\geq\frac{1}{4}\int\frac{f^{\prime\prime 2}}{f}\geq\frac{1}{9}\int\frac{f^{\prime 4}}{f^{3}}.

Back to the proof of (
20.1
), write
g=\sqrt{f}
, the goal is

(20.5)

\int g^{\prime 2}\leq\frac{1}{K_{*}}\int g^{2}(\log g^{2})^{\prime\prime 2}.

But

\displaystyle\int g^{2}(\log g^{2})^{\prime\prime 2}

\displaystyle=4\int g^{2}\left(\frac{g^{\prime\prime}}{g}-\frac{g^{\prime 2}}{g^{2}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=4\int g^{\prime\prime 2}-8\int\frac{g^{\prime\prime}g^{\prime 2}}{g}+4\int\frac{g^{\prime 4}}{g^{2}}.

Now the relevant integration by parts reads

\int\frac{g^{\prime\prime}g^{\prime 2}}{g}=\frac{1}{3}\int\frac{g^{\prime 4}}{g^{2}},

so

(20.6)

\int f(\log f)^{\prime\prime 2}=4\int g^{\prime\prime 2}+\frac{4}{3}\int\frac{g^{\prime 4}}{g^{2}}=4\int(\sqrt{f})^{\prime\prime 2}+\frac{1}{12}\int\frac{f^{\prime 4}}{f^{3}}.

Combining this with the usual Poincaré inequality on
\mathbb{S}^{1}
yields

\int f\Gamma_{2}(\log f)=\int f(\log f)^{\prime\prime 2}\geq 4\int(\sqrt{f})^{\prime\prime 2}\geq 4\int(\sqrt{f})^{\prime 2}=I(f),

which yields
K_{*}\geq 1
and also implies the log Sobolev inequality with constant 1.
Combining this, instead, with the better Poincaré inequality for even functions (that is, when
f
is
\pi
-periodic) yields

\int f\Gamma_{2}(\log f)\geq 4\int(\sqrt{f})^{\prime\prime 2}\geq 16\int(\sqrt{f})^{\prime 2}=4I(f),

which also implies log Sobolev with constant 4.

To summarise:

L_{*}(\mathbb{R}\!\mathbb{P}^{1})=L(\mathbb{R}\!\mathbb{P}^{1})=\lambda_{1}(\mathbb{R}\!\mathbb{P}^{1})=4,

and this implies the bound announced in the proof of Theorem
15.1
.

20.2.
Dimensions 3 and higher

For
d\geq 3
there are various ways to combine the Hessian and curvature estimates in order to estimate the best constant
K_{*}
in the inequality

(20.7)

\int\Gamma_{1}(\sqrt{f})\leq\frac{1}{K_{*}}\int f\Gamma_{2}(\log f).

Here I will combine the optimal spectral gap with a change of nonlinearity. So the chain of inequalities will be

(20.8)

\int\Gamma_{1}(\sqrt{f})\leq\frac{1}{\lambda_{1}}\int\Gamma_{2}(\sqrt{f})\leq\frac{1}{\mathaccent 869{K}\lambda_{1}}\int f\Gamma_{2}(\log f).

Note that
\int\Gamma_{1}(\sqrt{f})=(1/4)I(f)
, so this implies log Sobolev with constant
\mathaccent 869{K}\lambda_{1}/4
. Since
L_{*}\leq\lambda_{1}
,
\mathaccent 869{K}
can be at most 4.

Now

\int\Gamma_{2}(\log f)=\int f\|\nabla^{2}\log f\|^{2}_{{\rm HS}}+(d-2)\int f|\nabla\log f|^{2},

\int\Gamma_{2}(\sqrt{f})=\int\|\nabla^{2}\sqrt{f}\|^{2}_{{\rm HS}}+(d-2)\int|\nabla\sqrt{f}|^{2},

and since
\int f|\nabla\log f|^{2}=4\int|\nabla\sqrt{f}|^{2}
, it will be sufficient to show

(20.9)

\int\|\nabla^{2}\sqrt{f}\|^{2}_{{\rm HS}}\leq\frac{1}{\mathaccent 869{K}}\int f\|\nabla^{2}\log f\|_{{\rm HS}}^{2}

for some
\mathaccent 869{K}\leq 4
.

For this let us play with nonlinear changes of variables: writing
\varphi\circ f=\varphi(f)
, the three basic such formulas are

(20.10)

\chi^{\prime}(f)\Delta f=\Delta\chi(f)-\chi^{\prime\prime}(f)\Gamma_{1}(f),

(20.11)

\Gamma_{1}(\varphi(f))=(\varphi^{\prime})^{2}(f)\,\Gamma_{1}(f),

(20.12)

\Gamma_{2}(\varphi(f))=(\varphi^{\prime})^{2}(f)\,\Gamma_{2}(f)+2\varphi^{\prime}(f)\,\varphi^{\prime\prime}(f)\Gamma\Gamma(f)+(\varphi^{\prime\prime})^{2}(f)\,\Gamma_{1}(f)^{2},

where I write indifferently
\Gamma_{i}(f)=\Gamma_{i}(f,f)
,
\Gamma_{1}=\Gamma
, and

\Gamma\Gamma(f)=\frac{1}{2}\Gamma\bigl(f,\Gamma(f,f)\bigr)

(morally this is
\langle\nabla^{2}f\cdot\nabla f,\nabla f\rangle
).

Dominique Bakry has been virtuose in showing the power of these local identities, combined with a fourth, integral, formula:

(20.13)

\int\psi(f)(\Delta f)^{2}=\int\Bigl[\psi(f)\,\Gamma_{2}(f)+3\psi^{\prime}(f)\,\Gamma\Gamma(f)+\psi^{\prime\prime}(f)\,\Gamma(f)^{2}\Bigr]^{2}.

(Notice that
\psi(f)=1/f
in dimension 1 reduces to (
20.2
).) Let us apply the above formula (
20.12
) with
f=g^{2}
and
\varphi(g)=\log g
:

\Gamma_{2}(\log g)=\frac{1}{g^{2}}\Gamma_{2}(g)-\frac{2}{g^{3}}\Gamma\Gamma(g)+\frac{1}{g^{4}}\Gamma(g)^{2};

or equivalently

f\Gamma_{2}(\log f)=4g^{2}\Gamma_{2}(\log g)=4\Gamma_{2}(g)-8\frac{\Gamma\Gamma(g)}{g}+4\frac{\Gamma(g)^{2}}{g^{2}};

using
\Gamma\Gamma(\log f)=8\,\Gamma\Gamma(\log g)=8\,(\Gamma\Gamma(g)/g^{3}-\Gamma(g)^{2}/g^{4})
and
\Gamma(\log f)^{2}=16\,\Gamma(\log g)^{2}
, we obtain

(20.14)

\int\Gamma_{2}(g)=\frac{1}{4}\int f\Gamma_{2}(\log f)+\frac{1}{4}\int f\Gamma\Gamma(\log f)+\frac{1}{16}\int f\Gamma(\log f)^{2}.

From (
20.13
),

\int f(\Delta\log f)^{2}=\int f\Gamma_{2}(\log f)+3\int f\Gamma\Gamma(\log f)+\int f\Gamma(\log f)^{2},

so

\int f\Gamma\Gamma(\log f)=\frac{1}{3}\left(\int f\bigl[(\Delta\log f)^{2}-\Gamma_{2}(\log f)\bigr]\right)-\frac{1}{3}\int f\Gamma(\log f)^{2}.

Plugging this back in (
20.14
),

\int\Gamma_{2}(\sqrt{f})=\frac{1}{4}\int f\Gamma_{2}(\log f)+\frac{1}{12}\left(\int f\bigl[(\Delta\log f)^{2}-\Gamma_{2}(\log f)\bigr]\right)-\frac{1}{12}\int f\Gamma(\log f)^{2}\\
+\frac{1}{16}\int f\Gamma(\log f)^{2};

that is

(20.15)

\int\Gamma_{2}(\sqrt{f})=\frac{1}{4}\int f\Gamma_{2}(\log f)+\frac{1}{12}\left(\int f\bigl[(\Delta\log f)^{2}-\Gamma_{2}(\log f)\bigr]\right)-\frac{1}{48}\int f\Gamma(\log f)^{2}.

The
{\rm CD}(d-2,d-1)
criterion yields

\Gamma_{2}(u)\geq\frac{(\Delta u)^{2}}{d-1}+(d-2)\,\Gamma(u),

or

(\Delta u)^{2}-\Gamma_{2}(u)\leq(d-2)[\Gamma_{2}(u)-(d-1)\Gamma(u)].

Plugging this back in (
20.15
) yields

\displaystyle\int\Gamma_{2}(\sqrt{f})

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{1}{4}+\frac{d-2}{12}\right)\int f\Gamma_{2}(\log f)-\frac{(d-2)(d-1)}{12}\int f\Gamma(\log f)-\frac{1}{48}\int f\Gamma(\log f)^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{d+1}{12}\right)\int f\Gamma_{2}(\log f)-\frac{(d-2)(d-1)}{12}\int f\Gamma(\log f)-\frac{1}{48}\int f\Gamma(\log f)^{2}.

This shows that

\mathaccent 869{K}\geq\frac{12}{d+1},\qquad\forall d\geq 2.

Combining this in (
20.8
) with
\lambda_{1}(\mathbb{R}\!\mathbb{P}^{d-1})=2d
yields for the optimal constant in (
20.7
)

(20.16)

K_{*}\geq\frac{24\,d}{d+1}

As
d
becomes large this is a poor estimate but for
d\leq 6
it improves on the obvious bound
L_{*}(\mathbb{R}\!\mathbb{P}^{d-1})\geq L_{*}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})
, and for
d=2
it yields again
K_{*}=16
(optimal), for
d=3
,
K_{*}\geq 18
(not bad) and it is anyway bigger than 16 for any
d
, completing the proof of Theorem
15.1
.

Bibliographical Notes

For
d=2
the key integration by parts (
20.2
) and its use appear in many places but the oldest reference that I am aware of is McKean
[
134
]
.

Formulas (
20.11
)–(
20.13
) can be found in various places of the literature on
\Gamma_{2}
and logarithmic Sobolev inequalities; e.g. Bakry
[
14
]
. These are also Lemmas 5.4.3 and 5.5.5 in
[
6
]
.

For higher dimensions there are many works about the estimate of the log Sobolev and differential Bakry–Émery constants
[
16
]
on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, but estimates about best constants on
\mathbb{R}\!\mathbb{P}^{d-1}
are much more rare: Fore increasingly precise results, see Saloff-Coste
[
156
]
, Fontenas
[
80
]
, Guillen–Silvestre
[
97
]
and Ji
[
113
]
.

21.
Exploiting gradient variations II: failed attempts

How to adapt or extend the good theory and tools from the diffusive case, to more general Boltzmann equation, possibly of fractional diffusion nature? Reviewed here are several attempts which did not succeed, but may possibly be partly saved.

21.1.
Decomposing the inequality

The desired inequality

\int\Gamma_{\beta}(\sqrt{F})\leq\frac{1}{K_{*}}\int F\Gamma_{1,\beta}(\log F)

involves at the same time a change of derivation order (1 more on the right hand side) and of nonlinearity (
\sqrt{\ }
vs
\log
). If a direct approach is elusive, one can try and handle one change at a time, decomposing in any of the following two ways:

(a)
K_{*}\geq\overline{K}\mathaccent 869{K}
, where
\overline{K}
,
\mathaccent 869{K}
are best in

(21.1)

\int\Gamma_{\beta}(\sqrt{F})\leq\frac{1}{\overline{K}}\int\Gamma_{1,\beta}(\sqrt{F})

(21.2)

\int\Gamma_{1,\beta}(\sqrt{F})\leq\frac{1}{\mathaccent 869{K}}\int F\,\Gamma_{1,\beta}(\log F).

(b)
K_{*}\geq\dot{K}\mathaccent 866{K}
, where
\dot{K}
,
\mathaccent 866{K}
are best in

(21.3)

\int\Gamma_{\beta}(\sqrt{F})\leq\frac{1}{\dot{K}}D_{\beta}(F)

(21.4)

D_{\beta}(F)\leq\frac{1}{\mathaccent 866{K}}\int F\,\Gamma_{1,\beta}(\log F).

Here

D_{\beta}(F)=\frac{1}{2}\iint\bigl(F(\sigma)-F(k)\bigr)\bigl(\log F(\sigma)-\log F(k)\bigr)\,\beta(k\cdot\sigma)\,dk\,d\sigma

is the dissipation of Boltzmann’s
H
functional along the operator
L_{\beta}
.
In all these inequalities,
F
is assumed even.

It turns out that two of the above constants are easy to estimate. From the linear discussion we recall that
\overline{K}\geq\lambda_{1}(\mathbb{R}\!\mathbb{P}^{d-1})=2d
(with equality in general). And from the elementary inequality
4(\sqrt{x}-\sqrt{y})^{2}\leq(x-y)\log(x/y)
we have
\dot{K}\geq 4
(with equality in general). This reduces the problem to the estimates of either
\mathaccent 869{K}
(as I have done in the diffusive case) or
\mathaccent 866{K}
(which in the diffusive case is just as tricky as estimating
K_{*}
).

21.2.
Nonlocal
\Gamma_{2}
changes of variables

Nonlinear changes of variables, both for
\Gamma
and
\Gamma_{2}
, proved powerful in the previous section and it is natural to try and repeat them in the nonlocal context of the Boltzmann collision operator. A seemingly good news is that all four formulas (
20.10
)–(
20.13
) have nonlocal analogues. The analogue of (
20.10
) is

(21.5)

\chi^{\prime}(f)L_{\beta}f=L_{\beta}(\chi(f))-\frac{1}{2}\int\Bigl(\chi(f(\sigma))-\chi(f(k))-\chi^{\prime}(f(k))\bigl[f(\sigma)-f(k)\bigr]\Bigr)\beta(k\cdot\sigma)\,d\sigma,

note that inside the large brackets the expression looks like
\chi^{\prime\prime}(f)[f(\sigma)-f(k)]^{2}
. The analogue of (
20.11
) is the rather tautological

(21.6)

\Bigl(\varphi(f(\sigma))-\varphi(f(k))\Bigr)^{2}=\left(\frac{\varphi(f(\sigma))-\varphi(f(k))}{f(\sigma)-f(k)}\right)^{2}\bigl(f(\sigma)-f(k)\bigr)^{2}.

The analogue of (
20.12
) is the less obvious

(21.7)

\Bigl|\Phi^{\prime}(f(\sigma))\nabla f(\sigma)-\Phi^{\prime}(f(k))\nabla f(k)\Bigr|^{2}_{k,\sigma}=\Phi^{\prime}(f(k))^{2}\,\bigl|\nabla f(\sigma)-\nabla f(k)\bigr|^{2}_{k,\sigma}\\
+2\Phi^{\prime}(f(k))\bigl[\Phi^{\prime}(f(\sigma))-\Phi^{\prime}(f(k))\bigr]\nabla f(\sigma)\cdot\bigl[\nabla f(\sigma)-P_{k\sigma}\nabla f(k)\bigr]\\
+\bigl[\Phi^{\prime}(f(\sigma))-\Phi^{\prime}(f(k))\bigr]^{2}\,|\nabla f(\sigma)|^{2}.

Finally the analogue of (
20.13
) is

(21.8)

\int\psi(f)\,L_{\beta}f\Delta f=\frac{1}{2}\iint\psi(f(k))\bigl|\nabla f(\sigma)-\nabla f(k)\bigr|^{2}_{k,\sigma}\beta(k\cdot\sigma)\,dk\,d\sigma\\
+\frac{1}{2}\iint\Bigl[2\psi^{\prime}(f(\sigma))\bigl[f(\sigma)-f(k)\bigr]^{2}+\bigl(\psi(f(\sigma))-\psi(f(k))\bigr)\Bigr]\\
\left(\frac{|\nabla f(\sigma)|^{2}}{2}-\frac{|\nabla f(k)|^{2}}{2}\right)\,\beta(k\cdot\sigma)\,dk\,d\sigma\\
